bound for
Y(\Scaff,\psi^{+},\psi^{-},P)
:

(11.10)

\begin{split}Y(\Scaff,\psi^{+},\psi^{-},P^{\prime}):=\sup_{\xi^{+}_{2N-1}}&\int{\varepsilon}^{|K(\bm{\Gamma}[\Base])|}\chi_{{\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}}^{+}(\Gamma^{+}[\Base])\chi_{{\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}}^{+}(\Gamma^{-}[\Base])\\
&\qquad\qquad\prod f_{{\mlq\mlq p\mrq\mrq},a}(\bm{\Gamma}_{\leq{\mlq\mlq p\mrq\mrq},a})f_{{\mlq\mlq y\mrq\mrq},a}(\bm{\Gamma}_{\leq{\mlq\mlq y\mrq\mrq},a})f_{{\mlq\mlq\xi\mrq\mrq},b,m}(\bm{\Gamma}_{\leq{\mlq\mlq\xi\mrq\mrq},b,m})\\
&\qquad\qquad\prod_{A\in Q}{\mathbf{1}}(y_{A}\text{ is }r\text{-connected})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\bm{\Gamma}_{D}.\end{split}

Implicitly the partition
Q
depends on
P^{\prime}
and
\Scaff
(through
{\mathcal{F}}
), but as these will be fixed
for the remainder of the section we simply write ‘Q’.

Lemma 11.1
.

The quantity
Y(\Scaff,\psi^{+},\psi^{-},P^{\prime})
defined above satisfies

Y(\Scaff,\psi^{+},\psi^{-},P^{\prime})\leq N^{C\|{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}\|}r^{-d|J^{+}_{clust}|}{\varepsilon}^{c\|{\mathcal{F}}\|_{+}}.

11.3.
Integrating out
\Gamma^{-}[\Base]

The first step we take towards the proof of Lemma
11.1

is to integrate out the
\Gamma^{-}
variables.

To do so we first write out more explicitly the constraints on the time variables
s_{a}
.
The constraints on
y_{A}
imply constraints on the time variables
s_{a}
when
a
is not of the form
\min(S)-1

for some
S\in Q
. In particular, we define the time constraints

f_{{\mlq\mlq s\mrq\mrq},a}(\bm{\Gamma}_{\leq{\mlq\mlq s\mrq\mrq},a}):=\begin{cases}{\mathbf{1}}_{[0,\tau]}(s_{a}),&a=\min(S)-1\text{ for some }S\in Q\\
{\mathbf{1}}(|y_{a^{\prime}}+(y_{a}+s_{a}p_{a})|\leq 2|S|r),&a+1\in S\in Q,a^{\prime}=\min(S).\end{cases}

To interate out the variables of
\Gamma^{-}
we ignore the term
\chi_{{\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}}^{+}(\Gamma^{-}[\Base])

(which is bounded by
1
) and use the product of time constraints
\prod f_{{\mlq\mlq s\mrq\mrq},a}
to
enforce
{\mathbf{1}}(y_{A}\text{ is }r\text{-connected})
for every
A\in Q
. We therefore bound

(11.11)

\begin{split}Y(\Scaff,P^{\prime})&\leq\sup_{\xi^{+}_{2N-1}}\int{\varepsilon}^{|K(\bm{\Gamma}[\Base])|}\chi_{{\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}}(\Gamma^{+}[\Base])\prod f_{{\mlq\mlq p\mrq\mrq},a}(\bm{\Gamma}_{\leq{\mlq\mlq p\mrq\mrq},a})f_{{\mlq\mlq y\mrq\mrq},a}(\bm{\Gamma}_{\leq{\mlq\mlq y\mrq\mrq},a})\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\prod f_{{\mlq\mlq\xi\mrq\mrq},b,m}(\bm{\Gamma}_{\leq{\mlq\mlq\xi\mrq\mrq},b,m})f_{{\mlq\mlq s\mrq\mrq},a}(\bm{\Gamma}_{\leq{\mlq\mlq s\mrq\mrq},a})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\bm{\Gamma}[\Base]\\
&\leq\sup_{\Gamma^{+}[\Base]}\int{\varepsilon}^{|K(\Gamma^{-}[\Base])|}\prod_{\Lambda[\Base]^{-}}f_{\lambda}(\bm{\Gamma}_{\leq\lambda})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\Gamma^{-}[\Base]\\
&\qquad\qquad\times\sup_{\xi^{+}_{2N-1}}\int{\varepsilon}^{|K(\Gamma^{+}[\Base])|}\chi_{{\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}}^{+}(\Gamma^{+}[\Base])\prod_{\Lambda[\Base]^{+}}f_{\lambda}(\bm{\Gamma}_{\leq\lambda}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\Gamma^{+}[\Base].\end{split}

In this subsection we count up the constraints on the
\Gamma^{-}
variables to bound the first
integral. To state the result we need to first define a few quantities.
Let
k^{+}
be the number of collisions in
\Gamma^{+}[\Base]
and
k^{-}
be the number of collisions
in
\Gamma^{-}[\Base]
. Given a partition
P\in{\mathcal{P}}(K(\bm{\Gamma}_{D}))
, we let

m(P)\subset K(\bm{\Gamma}_{D})
be the set

m(P)=\{\min S\mid S\in P\}

of “first” collisions in each set of
P
, while

M(P)=\{\max S\mid S\in P\}

is the set of “last” collisions. We write
m^{+}(P)
,
m^{-}(P)
and
M^{+}(P)
,
M^{-}(P)
for the number of
first and last collisions in
K^{+}
and
K^{-}
, respectively. Then we have the following bound

Lemma 11.2
.

Given a scaffold
\Scaff
and a partition
P^{\prime}
,
the following integral over
\Gamma^{-}
variables satisfies the bound

(11.12)

\begin{split}\sup_{\Gamma^{+}[\Base]}\int\prod_{\Lambda[\Base]^{-}}f_{\lambda}(\bm{\Gamma}_{\leq\lambda})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\Gamma^{-}[\Base]&\leq N^{C\|{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}\|}r^{-d|J^{+}_{clust}({\mathcal{F}}^{\prime})}\tau^{|m^{-}(Q)|}\\
&\qquad\times(E^{-1/2}r)^{k^{-}-|m^{-}(Q)|}(r^{-1}\min\{E^{(d-1)/2},1\})^{k^{-}-|M^{-}(Q)|}.\end{split}

Proof.

We integrate each variable independently using Lemma
4.13
. The resulting
bound follows from the following rules:

•

Each recollision pair
(s_{a},p_{a})
contributes
O(\log({\varepsilon}))
by Lemma
4.19
.

•

Each phase space variable labelled
{\mlq\mlq\xi^{\pm}_{m}\mrq\mrq}
contributes
O(N^{C})
, and there are

O(\|{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}\|)
such variables.

•

Each time variable
{\mlq\mlq s_{a-1}\mrq\mrq}
that is the first collision in a cluster
S\in Q(\psi;P^{\prime})

contributes at most
\tau
. There
are
|m^{-}(Q)|
such variables.

•

Each time variable
s_{a-1}
with
a\not\in m^{-}(Q)

cluster contributes
E^{-1/2}r
, where
E=|p_{\ell,0}|^{2}

is the kinetic energy of the path.

•

Each momentum variable
p_{a}
with
a\in M^{-}(P^{\prime})

contributes
C|S|^{d}
because of the
localization
r^{d}\exp(-c(r|S|^{-1}|q_{S}|)^{0.9})
. There are
M^{-}(P^{\prime})
such variables.
Of these,
|S|\leq 2
for all but at most
\|{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}\|
of the collisions, so the combinatorial
factor is at most
N^{C\|{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}\|}
.

•

Each momentum variable
p_{\ell,j}
that is not the last in its cluster and does not belong to a
recollision contributes
Cr^{-1}\min\{|p_{\ell,0}|^{d-1},1\}
by (
4.18
).
There are
N^{-}-M^{-}(P^{\prime})
such collisions.

•

Each first momentum variable
p_{\ell,0}
contributes
O(1)
.

•

Each
y_{\ell,j}
variables each contribute
O(1)
, except for the variables determined by delta
functions, which contribute
O(r^{-d})
.

∎

We split the analysis of the remaining integral into cases, depending on which of

F_{clust}
,
F_{rec}
,
F_{tube}
, or
F_{cone}
is most significant.

11.4.
The cluster dominated case

The first case we deal with is that

\frac{1}{2}(k^{+}+k^{-})-|Q|\geq\frac{1}{10}\|{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}\|_{+}.

This occurs in particular if

\sum_{S\in F^{\prime}_{clust}}|S\cap K^{+}_{all}|-1\geq\frac{1}{4}\|{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}\|_{+}

since each set
S\in F^{\prime}_{clust}
belongs to a cell
T\in Q
containing at least

2|S\cap K^{+}_{all}|
collisions.
In this case, the simpleminded analysis of the previous section works to obtain
a suitable bound for
Y(\Scaff,P^{\prime})
.

To integrate out the phase space variables we use the following
L^{1}
-type bound on the maximal
ladder function

\sup_{\xi}\int M_{k}(\xi,\eta)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\eta\leq N^{C}.

This bounds the contribution of the phase space variables by an overall factor of
N^{C\|{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}\|}
.
Then using the same accounting as in the proof of Lemma
11.2
we obtain

(11.13)

\begin{split}\sup_{\xi^{+}_{2N-1}}\int&\chi_{{\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}}^{+}(\Gamma^{+}[\Base])\prod_{\Lambda[\Base]^{+}}f_{\lambda}(\bm{\Gamma}_{\leq\lambda}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\Gamma^{+}[\Base]\\
&\leq(CN)^{C\|{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}\|}\tau^{m^{+}(Q)}(E^{-1/2}r)^{k^{+}-m^{+}(Q)}(r^{-1}\min\{E^{(d-1)/2},1\})^{k^{+}-M^{+}(Q)}.\end{split}

Now we combing this bound with
(
11.11
) and Lemma
11.2
and
simplify using

\tau^{|Q|}\leq\tau^{(k^{+}+k^{-})/2}\tau^{|Q|-(k^{+}+k^{-})/2}\leq\tau^{(k^{+}+k^{-})/2}{\varepsilon}^{(k^{+}+k^{-})/2-|Q|}

and

\sup_{E}\min\{E^{(d-2)/2},E^{-1/2}\}\leq 1

to conclude

(11.14)

\begin{split}Y(\Scaff,P^{\prime})&\leq(CN)^{C\|{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}\|}({\varepsilon}^{2}\tau)^{(k^{+}+k^{-})/2}{\varepsilon}^{(k^{+}+k^{-})/2-|Q|}r^{-d|\supp J^{+}_{clust}({\mathcal{F}}^{\prime})|},\end{split}

as desired.

11.5.
The recollision argument

The second case we deal with is that
|F_{rec}^{\prime}|\geq\frac{1}{4}\|{\mathcal{F}}\|_{+}
.
If
(a,b)\in F_{rec}
is a recollision event, then either
\{a,b\}\in P^{\prime}
or

\{a,b\}\subsetneq S\in Q
for some
S\in Q
. If the latter
situation occurs for at least half of the recollisions in
F_{rec}
, then we
are in the situation of the previous section. In this section we deal with the
former scenario.

To see where the decay comes from in this case, we observe that for each such
pair
\{a,b\}
the momentum variables essentially satisfy

|p_{b}-p_{b-1}+p_{a}-p_{a-1}|\leq 4\alpha r^{-1}

while also being constrained to an annulus of thickness
r^{-1}
. If
p_{a}
,
p_{a-1}
,
and
p_{b-1}
are chosen randomly, then there is only a small probability that
p_{b}
can
be chosen to both be on the annulus and satisfy the linear constraint above. Thus the key
estimate is the following bound, which we note is closely related to the “crossing estimate”
that is traditionally used to control diagrams.

Lemma 11.3
.

For any kinetic energy level
E\geq{\varepsilon}^{0.2}
, we have the estimate

\displaystyle\sup_{q_{0},q_{1},q_{2}\in{\mathbf{R}}^{d}}\int\big(\prod_{j=1}^{2}{\mathbf{1}}(||p_{j}|-E|\leq r^{-1})\big)

\displaystyle(1+|p_{j}-q_{j}|)^{-20d}{\mathbf{1}}(||q_{0}-p_{2}+p_{1}|-E|\leq r^{-1})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}p_{1}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}p_{2}

\displaystyle\leq C{\varepsilon}^{0.1}\big(\min\{E^{(d-1)/2},1\}r^{-1}\big)^{2}.

In particular,

\displaystyle\sup_{q_{0},q_{1},q_{2},q_{3}\in{\mathbf{R}}^{d}}\int\big(\prod_{j=1}^{3}{\mathbf{1}}(||p_{j}|-E|\leq r^{-1})\big)

\displaystyle(1+|p_{j}-q_{j}|)^{-20d}(r^{d}\exp(-c(r|q_{0}-p_{2}+p_{1}-p_{3}|)^{0.5}))\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}p_{1}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}p_{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}p_{3}

\displaystyle\leq C{\varepsilon}^{0.1}\big(\min\{E^{(d-1)/2},1\}r^{-1}\big)^{2}.

Proof.

Let
A_{E,r}(z)
denote the shifted annulus

A_{E,r}(z):=\{p\in{\mathbf{R}}^{d}\mid||p-z|-E|\leq r^{-1}\},

We note that for
(p_{1},p_{2})
that contributes to the integrand,

p_{2}\in A_{E,r}(0)\cap A_{E,r}(q_{0}-p_{1})
. We split the integral
into two parts. In the first part,
|q_{0}-p_{1}|\geq{\varepsilon}^{0.5}
. In this
case, the intersection
A_{E,r}(0)\cap A_{E,r}(q-p_{1})
has volume
at most
(r^{-1}{\varepsilon}^{0.5})E^{(d-1)/2}r^{-1}
, from which we gain
the extra factor of
{\varepsilon}^{0.1}
. The second integral involves the
constraint
|q_{0}-p_{1}|\leq{\varepsilon}^{0.5}
, which also contributes an
extra factor of
{\varepsilon}^{0.1}
in the integration over
p_{1}
.

For the proof of the second bound, we simply observe that
the constraint
p_{3}\in A_{E,r}(0)
and the exponential
localization of
p_{3}
enforces that

p_{2}\in A_{E,r}(q_{0}-p_{1})
, and we use the argument for the first bound.
The integral in
p_{3}
produces only a constant factor.
∎

To apply Lemma
11.3
, we need to make some observations about the
momentum variables
p_{a}
. If
[\ell_{1},\ell_{2}]\in\pi({\mathcal{I}}^{stick}(D))
is a stick,
then
|p_{\ell_{1}-1,k_{\ell_{1}-1}}-p_{\ell_{2}+1,0}|\leq CN^{C}r^{-1}
. For each

\{a,b\}\in F_{rec}
, if
a-1
is of the form
(\ell,0)
then
\ell-1
must be the endpoint
of a “stick” interval (otherwise it would be a ladder interval, but then the first collision
in the segment must be a rung and not a recollision). Thus we can isolate the momentum
variables of the form
p_{a},p_{a-1},p_{b},p_{b-1}
for
\{a,b\}\in F_{rec}\cap P^{\prime}
and integrate
over these variables
first
, leaving the ordering on
\Lambda_{D}^{+}
otherwise unchanged.

We then use the following integral bound, which follows from Lemma
11.3

above.

Lemma 11.4
.

Let
\{(a_{i},b_{i})\}_{i=1}^{m}\subset\binom{[K]}{2}
be a collection of disjoint pairs
of indices in
[K]
satisfying
a_{i}+1<b_{i}
for some
K\in{\mathbb{N}}
.
(meaning that
\{a_{i},b_{i}\}\cap\{a_{j},b_{j}\}=\varnothing
for
i\not=j
).
Then, for any
E\geq r/\sigma
,

(11.15)

\begin{split}\int\prod_{i=1}^{K}{\mathbf{1}}(||p_{i}|-E|\leq r^{-1})\prod_{j=1}^{m}r^{d}&\exp(-c(r|p_{a_{i}}-p_{a_{i}-1}+p_{b_{i}}-p_{b_{i}-1}|)^{0.5})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\mathbf{p}}\\
&\leq C^{K}r^{-m/100}\big(\min\{E^{(d-1)/2},1\}r^{-1}\big)^{K-m}.\end{split}

The additional required ingredient is the following elementary combinatorial
fact.

Lemma 11.5
.

Let
{\mathcal{S}}\subset[K]
be a collection of sets
S
containing four elements each,
such that any index
j\in[K]
is contained in at most two sets.
Then there exists subsets
T(S)\subset S
for each
S\in{\mathcal{S}}

such that
|T(S)|\geq 1
for every
S\in{\mathcal{S}}
, the sets
T(S)
are
disjoint, and
|T(S)|=3
for at least
|{\mathcal{S}}|/4
sets.

Proof of Lemma
11.4
using Lemma
11.5
.

We apply Lemma
11.5

to the collection of sets
{\mathcal{S}}=\{\{a_{i}-1,a_{i},b_{i}-1,b_{i}\}\}_{i=1}^{m}
.
Then we reorder
[K]
so that the elements of
T(S)
are last
and the rest of the elements are first.
The triple sets contribute
{\varepsilon}^{0.1}\big(\min\{E^{(d-1)/2},1\}r^{-1}\big)^{2}

and the singletons constribute
O(1)
from the stretched exponential localization.
Every other index that is not contained in some
T(S)
contributes

\min\{E^{(d-1)/2},1\}r^{-1}
.
∎

From Lemma
11.4
we gain an additional improvement

{\varepsilon}^{c|F_{rec}|}
over the bound (
11.14
). We therefore obtain

(11.16)

\begin{split}Y(D,\psi,P^{\prime})&\leq(CN)^{C\|{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}\|}\max_{P^{\prime}\leq P(\psi)}({\varepsilon}^{2}\tau)^{(k^{+}+k^{-})/2}{\varepsilon}^{c|\supp^{+}F^{\prime}_{rec}|}r^{-d|\supp F_{sing}|}\\
&\leq(CN{\varepsilon}^{\beta})^{C\|{\mathcal{F}}\|}r^{-d|\supp J^{+}_{clust}({\mathcal{F}}^{\prime})|}.\end{split}

We conclude this subsection with the proof of the combinatorial lemma.

Proof of Lemma
11.5
.

We build the sets
T(S)
by a greedy algorithm. At the
j
-th step we keep track
of the collection of “completed” sets
{\mathcal{C}}_{j}\subset{\mathcal{S}}
, a set
of “remaining” sets
{\mathcal{R}}_{j}\subset{\mathcal{S}}
, a subset choice function

T_{j}:{\mathcal{C}}_{j}\to 2^{[K]}
and a “token count” function

f_{j}:{\mathcal{S}}\to{\mathbb{Z}}
. We also define the “inaccessible elements”

I_{j}\subset[K]
to be
\bigcup_{S\in{\mathcal{C}}_{j}}T(S)
. These are the elements that have
been chosen to be part of a subset already by the
j
th step.

At the initial step we set
{\mathcal{C}}_{0}=\varnothing,{\mathcal{R}}_{0}={\mathcal{S}}
,
and
f_{j}(S)=0
for all
S
. At each step we maintain the invariants that

\sum_{{\mathcal{S}}}f_{j}(S)\leq 0
, that for each
S\in{\mathcal{R}}_{j}
,

|S\setminus I_{j}|\geq 4-f_{j}(S)
, and
f_{j}(S)=0
if
S\cap I_{j}=\varnothing
.

At the
j
-th step we arbitrarily pick a set
S\subset{\mathcal{R}}_{j}
minimizing

|S\setminus I_{j}|
. If such
S
has
|S|=4
, then we choose

T(S)\subset S
arbitrariliy, set
{\mathcal{C}}_{j+1}={\mathcal{C}}_{j}\cup\{S\}

and
{\mathcal{R}}_{j+1}={\mathcal{C}}_{j}\setminus\{S\}
. We update the token
count function by setting
f_{j+1}(S)=-3
and

f_{j+1}(S^{\prime})=f_{j}(S^{\prime})+|S^{\prime}\cap T(S)|
for every
S^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{R}}_{j}
. It
is clear that the token count updated in this way maintains the invariants above.

If the minimal
|S\setminus I_{j}|
has
|S\setminus I_{j}|<4
there are three cases to
consider. The first is that
|S\setminus I_{j}|=3
and there exists

S^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{R_{j}}}
such that
S^{\prime}\setminus I_{j}=S\setminus I_{j}
. In this case
we choose two elements
a
and
b
arbitrarily from
S\setminus I_{j}

and set
T_{j+1}(S)=\{a\}
and
T_{j+1}(S^{\prime})=\{b\}
. Because both
a
and
b

belong to
S
and
S^{\prime}
, and no element is contained in more than one set,
there are no other sets
S^{\prime\prime}
containing
a
or
b
. We then simply
update
{\mathcal{R}}_{j+1}={\mathcal{R}}_{j}\setminus\{S,S^{\prime}\}

and
{\mathcal{C}}_{j+1}={\mathcal{C}}_{j}\cup\{S,S^{\prime}\}
, and
f_{j+1}=f_{j}
.

The second case is that
|S\setminus I_{j}|=3
and there does not exist another

S^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{R}}_{j}
such that
S^{\prime}\setminus I_{j}=S\setminus I_{j}
.
In this case we set
T(S)=S\setminus I_{j}
and proceed with the token update
rule
f_{j+1}(S)=-3
,
f_{j+1}(S^{\prime})=f_{j}(S)+|S^{\prime}\cap T(S)|
.

The final case is that
|S\setminus I_{j}|<2
. In this case we choose an element

a\in S\setminus I_{j}
arbitrarily and assign
T(S)=\{a\}
. We update the
token function by setting
f_{j+1}(S)=f_{j}(S)-1
and
f_{j+1}(S^{\prime})=f_{j}(S^{\prime})+1

if there exists
S^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{R}}_{j}
such that
a\in S^{\prime}
.

Repeating this process until
{\mathcal{R}}_{j}
is empty, we obtain a collection of
disjoint sets
T(S)\subset S
for each
S\in{\mathcal{S}}
. It remains to check
that
|T(S)|=3
for at least
|{\mathcal{S}}|/4
sets. To see this we observe
that
f(S)=-3
for such sets, whereas
f(S^{\prime})\geq 1
if
|T(S^{\prime})|=1

and
\sum f(S)\leq 0
.
∎

11.6.
The tube event case

The third case we consider is that
|\supp^{+}F^{\prime}_{tube}|\geq\|{\mathcal{F}}\|_{+}/4
. In
this case at least some constant fraction of the tube events

(a,b)\in F_{tube}
satisfy
b\not\in\supp F_{rec}
.
and
b-1\not\in M(P^{\prime})
. If not, we can reduce to one of the previous cases.
In this case, we will introduce an extra constraint on either the time variable

s_{b-1}
or the momentum variable
p_{b-1}
.
In this section we argue that the bound

(11.17)

\begin{split}Y(\Scaff,P^{\prime})&\leq(CN)^{C\|{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}\|}{\varepsilon}^{\beta|\supp^{+}F^{\prime}_{tube}|}r^{-d|\supp J^{+}_{clust}({\mathcal{F}}^{\prime})|},\end{split}

holds for some
\beta>0
.

Recall that a tube event occurs when
y_{b}
lies in the tube

T(y_{a},p_{a}):=\{z\in{\mathbf{R}}^{d}\mid\text{ there exists }s\in{\mathbf{R}}\text{ such that }|y_{a}+sp_{a}-z|\leq N^{8}r\}.

Indeed, if

|p_{b-1}-p_{a}|\geq{\varepsilon}^{0.6}
, then we have

\sup_{y_{a},p_{a},p_{b-1}}\int{\mathbf{1}}(y_{b-1}+s_{b-1}p_{b-1}\in T(y_{a},p_{a}))\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s_{b-1}\leq{\varepsilon}^{-0.6}N^{8}r.

And this is an improvement over
\tau
by a factor of
N^{8}{\varepsilon}^{0.4}
.
On the other hand, if
|p_{b-1}-p_{a}|\leq{\varepsilon}^{0.6}
, then we gain an improvement
in the
p_{b-1}
variable.

The only challenge with implementing this idea is that there may be many
pairs of the form
(a_{i},b)\in F_{tube}
with
a_{i}<b
. In this case the constraints
“collide” and naively there is only a gain of one factor of
{\varepsilon}^{\beta}
.

A very simplistic and extraordinarily suboptimal solution to this problem is as follows:
If for some
b
there are at most
m
tuples of the form
(a,b)
(with
m
to be chosen
momentarily, in fact
m=60d
works), then we still
gain a factor of
{\varepsilon}^{\beta/m}
for each pair in
F^{\prime}_{tube}
. If on the other hand
for some
b
there are at least
m
pairs
(a_{i},b)
then we integrate over all possible
values of
y_{b}
at a cost of
{\varepsilon}^{-C}
(with
C<3d
). Then, having conditioned on the value of
y_{b}
,
we gain a factor of
{\varepsilon}^{\beta}
on the variables determining
(y_{a},p_{a})
by splitting
into cases as follows:

•

In the case
|y_{a}-y_{b}|\leq{\varepsilon}^{-0.5}r
, we gain a factor of
{\varepsilon}^{0.3}

in the integration over
s_{a-1}
.

•

In the case
|y_{a}-y_{b}|>{\varepsilon}^{-0.5}r
we gain a factor of
{\varepsilon}^{0.1}
in the
integration over the
p_{a}
variable.

Either way, each such collision contributes an additional factor of
{\varepsilon}^{0.1}
. If

m>60d
, then
{\varepsilon}^{0.1(m/2)}<{\varepsilon}^{3d}
, so that
{\varepsilon}^{0.1m}{\varepsilon}^{-C}<{\varepsilon}^{\beta m}
,
and again it can be seen that each tube incidence in
F^{\prime}_{tube}
contributes a factor of
{\varepsilon}^{\beta}
.

11.7.
Cone events

The final case we consider is that
|\supp^{+}F^{\prime}_{cone}|\geq\|{\mathcal{F}}\|_{+}/4
.
Recall that in a cone event
(a,b)\in F_{cone}
, there exists
some
v\in{\mathbf{R}}^{d}
such that

\displaystyle||v|^{2}/2-E|

\displaystyle\leq\alpha^{2}N^{10}E^{1/2}r^{-1}

\displaystyle||v-q_{a}|^{2}/2-E|

\displaystyle\leq\alpha^{2}N^{10}E^{1/2}r^{-1}

\displaystyle||v+q_{b}|^{2}/2-E|

\displaystyle\leq\alpha^{2}N^{10}E^{1/2}r^{-1}

\displaystyle|y_{a}+tv-y_{b}|

\displaystyle\leq\alpha^{2}N^{10}r,

and the constraint that this does not hold for
v=p_{a}
implies that
there must exist some exterior collision between
a
and
b
.

For each of these cone events, if neither of the following constraints holds:

\displaystyle|y_{a}-y_{b}|

\displaystyle\leq{\varepsilon}^{-0.8}r

\displaystyle|\frac{y_{a}-y_{b-1}}{|y_{a}-y_{b-1}|}-p_{b-1}|

\displaystyle\leq{\varepsilon}^{0.2},

then the momentum variable
p_{b}
is constrained to be on the intersection
of the annulus
A_{E,r}(0)\cap A_{E,\delta}(E^{1/2}\frac{y_{a}-y_{b}}{y_{a}-y_{b}})
.
In any such case there is a gain of a factor of
{\varepsilon}^{\beta}
in one of the variables

s_{b-1}
,
p_{b-1}
, or
p_{b}
. Now if there are too many pairs of the form
(a_{i},b)

with some fixed
b
we can spend a factor of
{\varepsilon}^{-C}
to deduce the values of

(y_{b},p_{b-1},p_{b})
and obtain factors of
{\varepsilon}^{\beta}
instead on

(s_{a-1},p_{a-1},p_{a})
(the cone event is symmetric so the same geometric argument
works in this reverse case).
Either way we have

(11.18)

\begin{split}Y(\Scaff,P^{\prime})&\leq(CN)^{C\|{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}\|}{\varepsilon}^{\beta|\supp^{+}F^{\prime}_{tube}|}r^{-d|\supp J^{+}_{clust}({\mathcal{F}}^{\prime})|},\end{split}

and this concludes the final case of the proof of Proposition
10.1
.

12.
Analysis of the ladder superoperator

In this section we prove some estimates on the ladder superoperator and its variants that appear
in the diagrammatic decomposition of
{\widetilde{{\mathcal{E}}}}_{N\tau}
.

Recall that the single segment ladder superoperator
{\mathcal{L}}
is defined by

{\mathcal{L}}[A]:=\int\mathinner{|{\xi_{0}^{-}}\rangle}\mathinner{\langle{\xi_{0}^{+}}|}\mathinner{\langle{\xi_{1}^{-}|A|\xi_{1}^{+}}\rangle}\Xi(\Gamma){\mathbf{E}\,}_{lad}\begin{smallmatrix}\mathinner{\langle{\xi_{1}^{+}|O_{\omega^{+}}|\xi_{0}^{+}}\rangle}\\
\mathinner{\langle{\xi_{1}^{-}|O_{\omega^{-}}|\xi_{0}^{-}}\rangle}^{*}\end{smallmatrix}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\Gamma.

The ladder superoperator can also be expressed in terms of colored path operators.
Let
O_{lad}
be the operator

(12.1)

O_{lad}:=\sum_{k=0}^{k_{max}}\int_{\Omega_{k}(\tau)}\int_{({{\mathbf{R}}^{2d}})^{2}}\mathinner{|{\xi_{0}}\rangle}\mathinner{\langle{\xi_{1}}|}\chi_{\alpha,\sigma}(\omega;\xi_{0},\xi_{1})\mathinner{\langle{\xi_{0}|O_{\omega}^{\Psi^{+}_{rl}([k])}|\xi_{1}}\rangle}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\omega\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi_{0}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi_{1}.

Then

{\mathcal{L}}[A]={\mathbf{E}\,}O_{lad}^{*}AO_{lad}.

We define the
k
-extended ladder superoperator
{\mathcal{L}}_{k}
to be the analogue of

{\mathcal{L}}
formed from extended paths. We define the extended ladder operator

O_{lad,k}
by integrating over paths in
\Omega_{\alpha,\sigma}^{k}
(that is, paths
having
k
segments):

(12.2)

O_{lad,k}=\int_{\Omega_{\alpha,\sigma}^{k}}\Xi(\Gamma)O_{\Gamma}^{\Psi_{rl}^{+}(K(\Gamma))}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\Gamma.

Note that
O_{lad,1}=O_{lad}
(in the case
k=1
the path
\Gamma=(\xi_{0},\omega,\xi_{1})

and the expression expands to (
12.1
).
Then we define

(12.3)

\begin{split}{\mathcal{L}}_{k}[A]:={\mathbf{E}\,}O_{lad,k}^{*}AO_{lad,k}\\
&=\int_{\Omega_{\alpha,\sigma}^{k}\times\Omega_{\alpha,\sigma}^{k}}\Xi(\bm{\Gamma}){\mathbf{E}\,}_{lad}O_{\Gamma^{-}}^{*}AO_{\Gamma^{+}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\bm{\Gamma}.\end{split}

The ladder superoperator is an essential
building block that we use in our expansion of the superoperator
{\widetilde{{\mathcal{E}}}}_{N\tau}
.
Analytically it will appear in the form of the
ladder function

(12.4)

L_{k}\big(\begin{smallmatrix}\xi,&\eta\\
\xi^{\prime},&\eta^{\prime}\end{smallmatrix}\big):=\mathinner{\langle{\eta|{\mathcal{L}}_{k}[\mathinner{|{\xi}\rangle}\mathinner{\langle{\xi^{\prime}}|}]|\eta^{\prime}}\rangle}.

In our analysis of
{\widetilde{{\mathcal{E}}}}_{N\tau}
we need a bound on the
maximal ladder function
,

(12.5)

M_{k}(\xi,\eta):=\sup_{\xi^{\prime},\eta^{\prime}\in T_{N^{6},\tau}(\xi,\eta)}|L_{k}\big(\begin{smallmatrix}\xi,&\eta\\
\xi^{\prime},&\eta^{\prime}\end{smallmatrix}\big)|,

where

T_{\delta,\tau}(\xi,\eta)=\bigcup_{|s|\leq\tau}\{(\xi^{\prime},\eta^{\prime})\in({{\mathbf{R}}^{2d}})^{2}\mid\xi\text{ is }(s,\delta)\text{-twinned to }\xi^{\prime}\text{ and }\eta\text{ is }(s,\delta)\text{-twinned to }\eta^{\prime}\}.

The main result of this section is the following bound on the maximal ladder function.

Lemma 12.1
.

The maximal ladder function defined in (
12.5
) satisfies, for
k\leq N
,

\sup_{\xi}\int M_{k}(\xi,\eta)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\eta\leq CN^{20d}.

We will prove Lemma
12.1
by understanding the mapping properties
of the superoperator
{\mathcal{L}}_{k}
itself.

12.1.
The semigroup property for
{\mathcal{L}}_{k}

The first step to understandin the superoperator
{\mathcal{L}}_{k}
is to compare it to the iterated
ladder superoperator
{\mathcal{L}}^{k}
defined by the recursion

{\mathcal{L}}^{k+1}[A]={\mathcal{L}}[{\mathcal{L}}^{k}[A]]
and
{\mathcal{L}}^{1}={\mathcal{L}}
.
The following lemma shows that these superoperators are very close when applied to
operators with good support.

Lemma 12.2
.

If
A
is an operator with good support and
k\leq N
then

\|{\mathcal{L}}_{k}[A]-{\mathcal{L}}^{k}[A]\|_{op}\leq C{\varepsilon}^{100}\|A\|_{op}.

Proof.

By Lemma (
5.1
) we can restrict the domain
of integration in (
12.3
) to paths which are
N^{4}
-complete,
up to a loss of
{\varepsilon}^{100}
in the operator norm.
Applying the same reasonin to the iterated ladder
{\mathcal{L}}^{k}[A]
, we see that it suffices
to bound

\int_{\Omega_{\alpha,\sigma}^{k}\times\Omega_{\alpha,\sigma}^{k}}\Xi(\bm{\Gamma}){\mathbf{1}}(\bm{\Gamma}\textrm{ is }N^{4}-\text{complete})[{\mathbf{E}\,}_{lad}O_{\Gamma^{-}}^{*}AO_{\Gamma^{+}}-{\mathbf{E}\,}_{local-lad}O_{\Gamma^{-}}^{*}AO_{\Gamma^{+}}]\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\bm{\Gamma},

where
{\mathbf{E}\,}_{local-lad}
only includes ladder partitions with rungs
(a,b)

satisfing
\ell(a)=\ell(b)
. These ladder partitions are precisely what are involved
in the iterated operator
{\mathcal{L}}^{k}
.
By the good support of
A
, we can further restrict the integral to paths satisfying

d_{r}(\xi_{0}^{+},\xi_{0}^{-})\leq N^{4}
as well as
|(\xi_{0}^{\pm})_{p}|\geq{\varepsilon}^{0.2}

(up to an error on the order
{\varepsilon}^{100}\|A\|
). For such paths
we can use (
6.3
) to see that

|t_{a}-t_{b}|\leq N^{10}{\varepsilon}^{-0.2}r
for all ladder rungs
(a,b)
.
Since
N^{10}{\varepsilon}^{-0.2}r\leq\sigma
and no collisions occur within
\sigma

of a segment boundary
jN\tau
, it follows that
\ell(a)=\ell(b)

for each such ladder rung, and the integrand above is identically zero.
∎

As a consequence of Lemma
12.2
and Proposition
4.10
, we
can conclude that for operators
A
with good support and
k\leq c{\varepsilon}^{-0.1}
,

(12.6)

\|{\mathcal{L}}_{k}[A]-{\mathcal{E}}_{\tau}^{k}[A]\|_{op}\leq C{\varepsilon}^{2.1}\tau k\|A\|_{op}.

12.2.
Trace norm bounds of the ladder superoperator

The quantity we would like to control involves an integral of the maximal ladder function. Thus
far we have only proven bounds in the operator norm, which is useful for controlling
pointwise values of the ladder function. To control such integrals we will need bounds for
the trace-norm of
{\mathcal{L}}_{k}[\rho]
.

Recall that the trace-norm of an operator
\rho
is defined by duality against the operator norm,

\|\rho\|_{tr}:=\sup_{\|A\|_{op}=1}\trace[A^{*}\rho].

Operators for which this norm is finite are called trace-class operators.
Using this trace duality pairing,
the channel
{\mathcal{E}}_{s}
is dual to the channel
{\mathcal{E}}_{-s}
:

\trace[({\mathcal{E}}_{s}[A])^{*}\rho]=\trace[A^{*}{\mathcal{E}}_{-s}[\rho]].

We write
{\mathcal{L}}_{k}^{*}
for the dual of the ladder superoperator
{\mathcal{L}}_{k}
.
This superoperator is obtained from the definition of
{\mathcal{L}}_{k}
by simply changing
i
to
-i
,
so it satisfies the same bounds as
{\mathcal{L}}_{k}
.

As a corollary we obtain the trace-norm bounds for the superoperator
{\mathcal{L}}_{k}
.

Lemma 12.3
.

Let
\Pi_{\delta}
be the multiplier operator

{\widehat{\Pi_{\delta}f}}(p)={\mathbf{1}}(|p|>\delta){\widehat{f}}(p),

Then for any bounded operator
A
, and
\delta>{\varepsilon}^{0.1}
,

\|\Pi_{\delta}{\mathcal{L}}_{k}[A]\Pi_{\delta}\|_{op}\leq(1+{\varepsilon}^{2.1}\tau)^{k}\|A\|_{op}.

By duality, if
\rho
is a trace-class operator satisfying
\rho=\Pi_{\delta}\rho\Pi_{\delta}
,

\|{\mathcal{L}}_{k}[\rho]\|_{tr}\leq(1+{\varepsilon}^{2.1}\tau)^{k}\|\rho\|_{tr},

Proof.

We first expand

\Pi_{\delta}{\mathcal{L}}_{k}[A]\Pi_{\delta}={\mathbf{E}\,}\Pi_{\delta}(O_{lad,k})^{*}A(O_{lad,k})\Pi_{\delta}.

By the conservation of kinetic energy condition on the paths in
O_{lad,k}
(coming from
the localization
\Xi(\Gamma)
in the integrand of (
12.2
)),
we have (up to negligible error)

O_{k}^{ladder}\Pi_{\delta}=\Pi_{\delta^{\prime}}O_{k}^{ladder}\Pi_{\delta}

with
\delta^{\prime}=\delta-{\varepsilon}^{0.8}
(say).
Then by the operator Cauchy-Schwarz inequality we have

\|\Pi_{\delta}{\mathcal{L}}_{k}[A]\Pi_{\delta}\|_{op}\leq\|A\|_{op}\|{\mathcal{L}}_{k}[\Pi_{\delta^{\prime}}]\|_{op}.

The operator
\Pi_{\delta^{\prime}}
is an operator with good support, as it can be written

\Pi_{\delta}=\int{\mathbf{1}}(|p|\geq{\varepsilon}^{0.2})\mathinner{|{\eta}\rangle}\mathinner{\langle{\xi}|}\mathinner{\langle{\eta|p}\rangle}\mathinner{\langle{p|\xi}\rangle}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}p\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\eta.

Therefore we can apply Lemma
12.2
and Proposition
4.10
to estimate

\displaystyle\|{\mathcal{L}}_{k}[\Pi_{\delta}]\|_{op}

\displaystyle\leq\|{\mathcal{L}}^{k}[\Pi_{\delta}]\|_{op}+{\varepsilon}^{100}

\displaystyle\leq(1+{\varepsilon}^{2.1}\tau)^{k}.

The dual statement follows from the calculation

\|{\mathcal{L}}_{k}[\rho]\|_{tr}=\sup_{\|A\|_{op}=1}\trace[A^{*}{\mathcal{L}}_{k}[\rho]]=\trace[(\Pi_{\delta}{\mathcal{L}}_{k}^{*}[A^{*}]\Pi_{\delta})\rho].

∎

Corollary 12.4

(
L^{1}
-type bound on ladder functions)
.

Let
L_{k}
be the ladder function defined in (
12.4
) and let
\rho\leq N^{8}
. Suppose
moreover that
|\xi_{p}|\geq 2{\varepsilon}^{0.1}
. Then

\int|L_{k}\big(\begin{smallmatrix}\xi,&\eta\\
\xi,&\eta\end{smallmatrix}\big)|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\eta\leq C(1+{\varepsilon}^{2.1}\tau)^{k}.

Proof.

Fix
\xi,\xi^{\prime}\in{{\mathbf{R}}^{2d}}
, and define the phase
\theta(\eta,\eta^{\prime})
so that

e^{i\theta(\eta)}L_{k}\big(\begin{smallmatrix}\xi,&\eta\\
\xi,&\eta\end{smallmatrix}\big)=|L_{k}\big(\begin{smallmatrix}\xi,&\eta\\
\xi,&\eta\end{smallmatrix}\big)|.

Let
B
be the operator

B=\int e^{-i\theta(\eta)}\mathinner{|{\eta}\rangle}\mathinner{\langle{\eta}|}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\eta

The operator
B
satisfies

\trace[B^{*}{\mathcal{L}}_{k}[\mathinner{|{\xi}\rangle}\mathinner{\langle{\xi}|}]]=\int|L_{k}\big(\begin{smallmatrix}\xi,&\eta\\
\xi,&\eta\end{smallmatrix}\big)|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\eta.

Moreover, the operator
\mathinner{|{\xi}\rangle}\mathinner{\langle{\xi^{\prime}}|}
satisfies

\Pi_{\delta}\mathinner{|{\xi}\rangle}\mathinner{\langle{\xi^{\prime}}|}\Pi_{\delta^{\prime}}=\mathinner{|{\xi}\rangle}\mathinner{\langle{\xi^{\prime}}|}
up to
negligible error. Therefore applying Lemma
12.3
for
the trace norm of
{\mathcal{L}}_{k}[\mathinner{|{\xi}\rangle}\mathinner{\langle{\xi^{\prime}}|}]
and
the Schur test to bound
\|B\|_{op}\leq C
, we have

\trace[B^{*}{\mathcal{L}}_{k}[\mathinner{|{\xi}\rangle}\mathinner{\langle{\xi^{\prime}}|}]]\leq\|B\|_{op}\|{\mathcal{L}}_{k}[\mathinner{|{\xi}\rangle}\mathinner{\langle{\xi^{\prime}}|}]\|_{tr}\leq C(1+{\varepsilon}^{2.1}\tau)^{k},

as desired.
∎

12.3.
The maximal ladder function

Now we approach the maximal ladder function
M_{k}
directly. Recall
that the maximal ladder function is defined by

M_{k}(\xi,\eta):=\sup_{\xi^{\prime},\eta^{\prime}\in T_{N^{6},\tau}(\xi,\eta)}|L_{k}\big(\begin{smallmatrix}\xi,&\eta\\
\xi^{\prime},&\eta^{\prime}\end{smallmatrix}\big)|.

We can simplify our task by applyin the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality
to the definition of
L_{k}
to obtain

(12.7)

\begin{split}|L_{k}\big(\begin{smallmatrix}\xi,&\eta\\
\xi^{\prime},&\eta^{\prime}\end{smallmatrix}\big)|&\leq|L_{k}\big(\begin{smallmatrix}\xi,&\eta\\
\xi,&\eta\end{smallmatrix}\big)|^{1/2}|L_{k}\big(\begin{smallmatrix}\xi^{\prime},&\eta^{\prime}\\
\xi^{\prime},&\eta^{\prime}\end{smallmatrix}\big)|^{1/2}\\
&\leq\max\{|L_{k}\big(\begin{smallmatrix}\xi,&\eta\\
\xi,&\eta\end{smallmatrix}\big)|,|L_{k}\big(\begin{smallmatrix}\xi^{\prime},&\eta^{\prime}\\
\xi^{\prime},&\eta^{\prime}\end{smallmatrix}\big)|\}.\end{split}

Applying this into the definition of the maximal ladder function
M_{k}
we obtain

M_{k}(\xi,\eta)\leq\sup_{\xi^{\prime},\eta^{\prime}\in T_{N^{6},\tau}(\xi,\eta)}|L_{k}\big(\begin{smallmatrix}\xi^{\prime},&\eta^{\prime}\\
\xi^{\prime},&\eta^{\prime}\end{smallmatrix}\big)|.

One tool we have to deal with the supremum in the maximal ladder function is the following
self-bounding property of the ladder functions.

Lemma 12.5
.

(12.8)

|L_{k}\big(\begin{smallmatrix}\xi,&\eta\\
\xi^{\prime},&\eta^{\prime}\end{smallmatrix}\big)|\leq\int|\langle{\xi^{\prime\prime}|\xi^{\prime}}\rangle||\langle{\eta^{\prime\prime}|\eta^{\prime}}\rangle||L_{k}\big(\begin{smallmatrix}\xi,&\eta\\
\xi^{\prime\prime},&\eta^{\prime\prime}\end{smallmatrix}\big)|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi^{\prime\prime}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\eta^{\prime\prime}

Proof.

This follows from applying the identity

\mathinner{|{\xi^{\prime}}\rangle}=\int\mathinner{|{\xi^{\prime\prime}}\rangle}\mathinner{\langle{\xi^{\prime\prime}|\xi^{\prime}}\rangle}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi^{\prime\prime}

into the definition of
L_{k}
and then using the triangle inequality.
∎

We will use the fact that
e^{i\tau\Delta/2}\mathinner{|{\xi}\rangle}\approx\mathinner{|{\xi_{s}}\rangle}

to derive a more convenient bound for
M_{k}
. Let
{\mathcal{U}}_{s}[A]:=e^{is\Delta/2}Ae^{-is\Delta/2}

be the free evolution channel. Then we define the time-shifted ladder function

L_{k,s}(\xi,\eta):=\mathinner{\langle{\eta|({\mathcal{U}}_{s}{\mathcal{L}}^{k}{\mathcal{U}}_{-s})[\mathinner{|{\xi}\rangle}\mathinner{\langle{\xi}|}]|\eta}\rangle}.

Lemma 12.6
.

The maximal ladder function
M_{k}
defined in (
12.5
) satisfies

M_{k}(\xi,\eta)\leq C\sup_{|s|\leq\tau}\int L_{k,s}(\xi^{\prime},\eta^{\prime})\exp(-cd_{r}(\xi,\xi^{\prime})^{0.5})\exp(-cd_{r}(\eta,\eta^{\prime})^{0.5})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi^{\prime}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\eta^{\prime}.

Proof.

First we use the definition of the twinning set to rewrite the maximal ladder function as follows:

\displaystyle M_{k}(\xi,\eta)\leq\sup_{|s|\leq\tau}\sup_{d_{r}(\xi^{\prime},\xi)\leq N^{6}}\sup_{d_{r}(\eta^{\prime},\eta)\leq N^{6}}|L_{k}\big(\begin{smallmatrix}\xi^{\prime}_{s},&\eta^{\prime}_{s}\\
\xi^{\prime}_{s},&\eta^{\prime}_{s}\end{smallmatrix}\big)|.

Now we use the identity

\mathinner{|{\xi^{\prime}_{s}}\rangle}=\int e^{-is\Delta/2}\mathinner{|{\xi^{\prime\prime}}\rangle}\mathinner{\langle{\xi^{\prime\prime}|e^{is\Delta/2}|\xi^{\prime}_{s}}\rangle}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\alpha
,
the definition of
{\mathcal{L}}_{k,s}
and the triangle inequality to bound

|L_{k}\big(\begin{smallmatrix}\xi^{\prime}_{s},&\eta^{\prime}_{s}\\
\xi^{\prime}_{s},&\eta^{\prime}_{s}\end{smallmatrix}\big)|.\leq\int|{\mathcal{L}}_{k,s}\big(\begin{smallmatrix}\xi^{\prime\prime},&\eta^{\prime\prime}\\
\xi^{\prime\prime},&\eta^{\prime\prime}\end{smallmatrix}\big)||\langle{\xi^{\prime\prime}|e^{is\Delta/2}|\xi_{s}}\rangle||\langle{\eta^{\prime\prime}|e^{is\Delta/2}|\eta_{s}}\rangle|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi^{\prime\prime}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\eta^{\prime\prime}.

Then the lemma follows from the bound

|\langle{\xi^{\prime\prime}|e^{is\Delta/2}|\xi_{s}}\rangle|\leq\exp(-cd_{r}(\xi^{\prime\prime},\xi)^{0.5})

and the self-bounding property of the ladder function.
∎

The time-shifted ladder functions are somewhat difficult to study directly because of the cutoff
functions
\chi(\omega;\xi,\eta)
present in the integral definition of
{\mathcal{L}}_{k}
.

We will work instead with the non-localized operators

O_{{\mathbf{s}},{\mathbf{p}}}=\mathinner{|{p_{k}}\rangle}\mathinner{\langle{p_{0}}|}e^{i\sum s_{j}|p_{j}|^{2}/2}\prod_{j=1}^{k}{\widehat{V}}(p_{j}-p_{j-1}),

and define

\overline{{\mathcal{L}}}[A]:=\int\rho(s_{0})\rho(s_{0}^{\prime})\rho(s_{k})\rho(s_{k}^{\prime}){\mathbf{E}\,}_{ladder}O_{{\mathbf{s}},{\mathbf{p}}}^{*}AO_{{\mathbf{s}}^{\prime},{\mathbf{p}}^{\prime}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\mathbf{s}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\mathbf{s}}^{\prime}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\mathbf{p}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\mathbf{p}}^{\prime},

where
\rho(s)
is the cutoff function enforcing
s\geq S
that is present in
\Xi
. In other words,
the difference between
{\mathcal{L}}
and
\overline{{\mathcal{L}}}
is simply the absence of the
spatial localizations of
\chi_{\alpha}(\omega;\xi,\eta)
.
Then we can define the simplified ladder function to be

{\overline{L}}(\xi,\eta):=|\langle{\eta|{\overline{{\mathcal{L}}}}[\mathinner{|{\xi}\rangle}\mathinner{\langle{\xi}|}]|\eta}\rangle|.

The same integration by parts argument
that proves Lemma
3.1
also proves that
\overline{{\mathcal{L}}}\approx{\mathcal{L}}
,
so we have the following bound.

Lemma 12.7
.

For
k\leq N
,

\sup_{\xi,\xi^{\prime},\eta,\eta^{\prime}}||L_{k}\big(\begin{smallmatrix}\xi_{s},&\eta_{s}\\
\xi_{s},&\eta_{s}\end{smallmatrix}\big)-\overline{L}_{k}\big(\begin{smallmatrix}\xi_{s},&\eta_{s}\\
\xi_{s},&\eta_{s}\end{smallmatrix}\big)|\leq{\varepsilon}^{100}.

Let
\overline{L}
be the ladder function derived from the superoperator
\overline{{\mathcal{L}}}
,

{\overline{L}}(\xi,\eta):=\mathinner{\langle{\eta|{\overline{{\mathcal{L}}}}[\mathinner{|{\xi}\rangle}\mathinner{\langle{\xi}|}]|\eta}\rangle}.

We use
{\overline{{\mathcal{L}}}}
to also define
{\overline{L}}_{k,s}
by

{\overline{L}}_{k,s}(\xi,\eta):=\mathinner{\langle{\eta|{\mathcal{U}}_{s}{\overline{{\mathcal{L}}}}^{k}{\mathcal{U}}_{-s}[\mathinner{|{\xi}\rangle}\mathinner{\langle{\xi}|}]|\eta}\rangle}=\mathinner{\langle{\eta|{\overline{{\mathcal{L}}_{s}}}^{k}[\mathinner{|{\xi}\rangle}\mathinner{\langle{\xi}|}]|\eta}\rangle},

where
{\overline{{\mathcal{L}}_{s}}}={\mathcal{U}}_{s}{\overline{{\mathcal{L}}}}{\mathcal{U}}_{-s}
.

Because the integration over
\xi^{\prime}
and
\eta^{\prime}
is only over a volume on the order
N^{C}\tau\leq{\varepsilon}^{-10}
,
we can combine Lemma
12.7
with Lemma
12.5
and Lemma
12.6

to obtain

(12.9)

M_{k}(\xi,\eta)\leq C\sup_{|s|\leq\tau}\int|\overline{L_{k,s}}(\xi^{\prime},\eta^{\prime})|\exp(-cd_{r}(\xi^{\prime},\xi)^{0.5})\exp(-cd_{r}(\eta^{\prime},\eta)^{0.5})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi^{\prime}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\eta^{\prime}+O({\varepsilon}^{50}).

Now we use the fundamental theorem of calculus to bound

(12.10)

|\overline{L_{k,s}}(\xi^{\prime},\eta^{\prime})|\leq|\overline{L_{k}}(\xi^{\prime},\eta^{\prime})|+\int_{-\tau}^{\tau}|\partial_{s}\overline{L_{k,s}}(\xi^{\prime},\eta^{\prime})|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s.

Next we compute the derivative of
\partial_{s}\overline{L_{k,s}}

(12.11)

\partial_{s}{\overline{{\mathcal{L}}}}_{k,s}=\partial_{s}({\overline{{\mathcal{L}}}}_{s})^{k}=\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}{\overline{{\mathcal{L}}}}_{s}^{k-j-1}\partial_{s}({\overline{{\mathcal{L}}}}_{s}){\overline{{\mathcal{L}}}}_{s}^{j},

and we observe that

\partial_{s}{\overline{{\mathcal{L}}}}_{s}[A]=\partial_{s}\int\rho(s_{0})\rho(s_{k})\rho(s_{0}^{\prime})\rho(s_{k}^{\prime}){\mathbf{E}\,}e^{-is\Delta/2}O_{{\mathbf{s}},{\mathbf{p}}}^{*}e^{is\Delta/2}Ae^{-is\Delta/2}O_{{\mathbf{s}}^{\prime},{\mathbf{p}}^{\prime}}e^{is\Delta/2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\mathbf{s}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\mathbf{p}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\mathbf{s^{\prime}}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\mathbf{p^{\prime}}}.

Given a time sequence
{\mathbf{s}}=(s_{0},\cdots,s_{k})
, define the shifted sequence

I_{s}{\mathbf{s}}=(s_{0}-s,s_{1},\cdots,s_{k-1},s_{k}+s)
. Then

e^{-is\Delta/2}O_{{\mathbf{s}},{\mathbf{p}}}^{*}e^{is\Delta/2}=O_{I_{s}{\mathbf{s}},{\mathbf{p}}},

so by then applying a change of variables we have

(12.12)

\partial_{s}{\overline{{\mathcal{L}}}}_{s}[A]=\int f_{s}(s_{0},s_{k},s_{0}^{\prime},s_{k}^{\prime}){\mathbf{E}\,}e^{-is\Delta/2}O_{{\mathbf{s}},{\mathbf{p}}}^{*}e^{is\Delta/2}Ae^{-is\Delta/2}O_{{\mathbf{s}}^{\prime},{\mathbf{p}}^{\prime}}e^{is\Delta/2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\mathbf{s}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\mathbf{p}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\mathbf{s^{\prime}}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\mathbf{p^{\prime}}},

where

f_{s}(s_{0},s_{k},s_{0}^{\prime},s_{k}^{\prime}):=\frac{d}{ds}\rho(s_{0}-s)\rho(s_{k}+s)\rho(s_{0}^{\prime}-s)\rho(s_{k}^{\prime}+s).

The key lemma is the following estimate on the superoperator
\partial_{s}{\overline{{\mathcal{L}}_{s}}}
.

Lemma 12.8
.

If
A
is an operator with good support, then

\|\partial_{s}{\overline{{\mathcal{L}}}}_{s}[A]\|_{op}\leq C{\varepsilon}^{2}|\log{\varepsilon}|^{C}\|A\|_{op}.

Proof.

Using the operator Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, it suffices to prove

\|\partial_{s}{\overline{{\mathcal{L}}}}_{s}[P_{\delta}]\|_{op}\leq C{\varepsilon}^{2},

where
\delta={\varepsilon}^{0.2}
and
P_{\delta}
is the wavepacket cutoff

P_{\delta}=\int_{|\xi_{p}|\geq\delta}\mathinner{|{\xi}\rangle}\mathinner{\langle{\xi}|}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi.

Then we use the same integration by parts from Lemma
3.1
to
approximate, for
g
smooth to scale
{\varepsilon}^{-1.1}
,

\int g({\mathbf{s}})O_{{\mathbf{s}},{\mathbf{p}}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\mathbf{s}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\mathbf{p}}=\int g({\mathbf{s}})\chi_{\alpha}(\omega;\xi,\eta)\mathinner{|{\eta}\rangle}\mathinner{\langle{\xi}|}\mathinner{\langle{\xi|O_{\omega}|\eta}\rangle}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\omega\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\eta

up to an error that is negligible in operator norm. Then we estimate

\|\partial_{s}{\overline{{\mathcal{L}}}}_{s}[A]\|_{op}
using the Schur test and the same
calculations as in the proof of Proposition
4.10
.
In particular, without any improvements from the presence of clusters in the partition or from
the presence of recollision or tube events, the argument of Proposition
4.10

yields

\|\partial_{s}{\overline{{\mathcal{L}}}}_{s}[A]\|_{op}\leq C\|A\|_{op}\sum_{k_{+},k_{-}=0}^{k_{max}}(C{\varepsilon}^{2}\tau|\log{\varepsilon}|^{C})^{(k_{+}+k_{-})/2}.

In summary, the bound above is obtained from the following considerations:
each pair
(a,b)
in the generalized ladder contributes
{\varepsilon}^{2}
from the two factors of
{\varepsilon}
,

\tau
from the choice of the time of the collision, and a factor of

r^{-1}
from the integration over the momentum annulus that is cancelled by a factor of
r

for the time variable of the second collision in the pair.

Now we obtain an improvement because of the presence of the function
f_{s}(s_{0},s_{k},s_{0}^{\prime},s_{k}^{\prime})
in the integrand.
This function is bounded by
\sigma^{-1}
and is supported on the set

\{s_{0}\leq\sigma\}\cup\{s_{k}\leq\sigma\}\cup\{s_{0}^{\prime}\leq\sigma\}\cup\{s_{k}^{\prime}\leq\sigma\}.

The effect is that the integration over the
s_{0}
variable (or
s_{0}^{\prime}
, or
s_{k}
, or
s_{k}^{\prime}
variable)
includes an additional factor of
\sigma^{-1}
and is integrated over a segment of length
\sigma

rather than
\tau
. The effect of this is to produce an additional improvement of
\sigma^{-1}\sigma/\tau=\tau^{-1}

over the bound given above. Since moreoever there must be at least one (and therefore at least
2
) collisions
for the integrand to be nonzero, we conclude that

\|\partial_{s}{\overline{{\mathcal{L}}}}_{s}[A]\|_{op}\leq C\|A\|_{op}\sum_{2\leq|k_{+}+k_{-}|\leq 2k_{max}}^{k_{max}}C\tau^{-1}(C{\varepsilon}^{2}\tau|\log{\varepsilon}|^{C})^{(k_{+}+k_{-})/2}\leq C{\varepsilon}^{2}|\log{\varepsilon}|^{C},

as desired.
∎

We now have the ingredients needed to prove Lemma
12.1
.

Proof of Lemma
12.1
.

We integrate (
12.9
) and apply (
12.10
) to obtain

\displaystyle\int M_{k}(\xi,\eta)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\eta

\displaystyle\leq C\int|{\overline{L_{k}}}(\xi^{\prime},\eta^{\prime})|\exp(-cd_{r}(\xi^{\prime},\xi)^{0.5})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi^{\prime}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\eta^{\prime}

\displaystyle\qquad+C\int_{-\tau}^{\tau}\int|\partial_{s}{\overline{L_{k,s}}}(\xi,\eta)|\exp(-cd_{r}(\xi,\xi^{\prime})^{0.5})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi^{\prime}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\eta\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s

The main term comes from
\xi^{\prime}
satisfying
d_{r}(\xi^{\prime},\xi)\leq N^{4}
, so we have

\displaystyle\int M_{k}(\xi,\eta)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\eta

\displaystyle\leq CN^{8d}\sup_{d_{r}(\xi^{\prime},\xi)\leq N^{4}}\int|{\overline{L_{k}}}(\xi^{\prime},\eta^{\prime})|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\eta^{\prime}

\displaystyle\qquad+CN^{8d}\sup_{d_{r}(\xi^{\prime},\xi)\leq N^{4}}\int_{-\tau}^{\tau}\int|\partial_{s}{\overline{L_{k,s}}}(\xi,\eta)|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\eta\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s+C{\varepsilon}^{100}.

The first term can be directly bounded using Corollary
12.4
.
The second term is bounded in the same way, by first deriving a trace-norm bound
for the operator
\partial_{s}{\overline{{\mathcal{L}}}}_{k,s}[\mathinner{|{\xi}\rangle}\mathinner{\langle{\xi}|}]
using
the duality argument of Lemma
12.3
and then
applying this to bound the integral over
\eta
exactly as in the proof of Corollary
12.4
.
∎

12.4.
Superoperators related to the ladder

In this section we transfer the bounds we obtained on the ladder superoperator
{\mathcal{L}}

to some other superoperators. The first of these is the stick superoperator

{\mathcal{S}}
, defined by

{\mathcal{S}}[A]:=\int\mathinner{|{\xi_{0}^{-}}\rangle}\mathinner{\langle{\xi_{0}^{+}}|}\mathinner{\langle{\xi_{1}^{-}|A|\xi_{1}^{+}}\rangle}\Xi(\Gamma){\mathbf{E}\,}_{stick}\begin{smallmatrix}\mathinner{\langle{\xi_{1}^{+}|O_{\omega^{+}}|\xi_{0}^{+}}\rangle}\\
\mathinner{\langle{\xi_{1}^{-}|O_{\omega^{-}}|\xi_{0}^{-}}\rangle}^{*}\end{smallmatrix}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\Gamma,

where now the expectation is over stick partitions (that is, ladders without rungs). This can also
be expressed in terms of colored operators,

{\mathcal{S}}[A]={\mathbf{E}\,}(O_{stick})^{*}AO_{stick},

where
O_{stick}
includes a sum over all stick colorings
\Psi_{rl}^{+,0}

instead of all ladders
\Psi_{rl}^{+}
. Since stick colorings are precisely ladder colorings
with no rungs,
O_{stick}={\mathbf{E}\,}O_{lad}
, so
{\mathcal{S}}[A]
can also be written

{\mathcal{S}}[A]=({\mathbf{E}\,}O_{lad})^{*}A({\mathbf{E}\,}O^{lad}).

Therefore all bounds on the operator norm of
{\mathcal{L}}[A]
are inherited by
{\mathcal{S}}[A]
,
since
({\mathbf{E}\,}O^{ladder})^{*}({\mathbf{E}\,}O^{ladder})\leq{\mathbf{E}\,}(O^{ladder})^{*}(O^{ladder})
.

This discussion can be generalized to handle ladder superoperators with a specified number of rungs.
Define the extended ladder operator with a specified number of rungs

O_{lad,k,=h}=\int_{\Omega_{\alpha,\sigma}^{k}}\Xi(\Gamma)O_{\Gamma}^{\Psi_{rl}^{+,h}(K(\Gamma))}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\Gamma.

Then we define

{\mathcal{L}}_{k,=h}[A]:={\mathbf{E}\,}O_{lad,k,h}^{*}AO_{lad,k,h}.

Note that
{\mathcal{L}}_{1,0}
is the stick superoperator considered above.
Let
L_{k,=h}
be the ladder functions associated to
{\mathcal{L}}_{k,=h}
,

(12.13)

L_{k,=h}\big(\begin{smallmatrix}\xi,&\eta\\
\xi^{\prime},&\eta^{\prime}\end{smallmatrix}\big):=|\langle{\eta|{\mathcal{L}}_{k,=h}[\mathinner{|{\xi}\rangle}\mathinner{\langle{\xi^{\prime}}|}]|\eta^{\prime}}\rangle|.

An application of Cauchy-Schwarz shows that

(12.14)

L_{k,=h}\big(\begin{smallmatrix}\xi,&\eta\\
\xi^{\prime},&\eta^{\prime}\end{smallmatrix}\big)\leq L_{k,=h}\big(\begin{smallmatrix}\xi,&\eta\\
\xi,&\eta\end{smallmatrix}\big)^{1/2}L_{k,=h}\big(\begin{smallmatrix}\xi^{\prime},&\eta^{\prime}\\
\xi^{\prime},&\eta^{\prime}\end{smallmatrix}\big)^{1/2}=L_{k,=h}(\xi,\eta)^{1/2}L_{k,=h}(\xi^{\prime},\eta^{\prime})^{1/2}.

Now we will compare the functions
L_{k,=h}
to
L_{k}
. The key observation is that

{\mathcal{Q}}(\Psi^{+,h}_{rl}(A),\Psi^{+}_{rl}(B))
is the set of ladder partitions with exactly

h
rungs (as all other partitions have singleton sets). Therefore
{\mathcal{L}}_{k,=h}
has the
remarkable representation

{\mathcal{L}}_{k,=h}[A]={\mathbf{E}\,}O_{lad,k,=h}^{*}AO_{lad,k}.

In particular,
L_{k,=h}(\xi,\eta)
can be written

L_{k,=h}=|{\mathbf{E}\,}\mathinner{\langle{\eta|O_{lad,k,=h}^{*}|\xi}\rangle}\mathinner{\langle{\xi|O_{lad,k}|\eta}\rangle}|,

so that an application of the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality shows

L_{k,=h}(\xi,\eta)\leq L_{k,=h}(\xi,\eta)^{1/2}L_{k}(\xi,\eta)^{1/2},

which upon rearranging becomes

L_{k,=h}(\xi,\eta)\leq L_{k}(\xi,\eta).

Therefore all bounds we obtained for the ladder function
L_{k}
apply to the ladder functions
L_{k,=h}

(including the case
h=0)
. In particular, for
k\leq N
,

(12.15)

\sup_{\xi}\int M_{k,=h}(\xi,\eta)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\eta\leq CN^{C}.

13.
The path integral

In this section we provide a more detailed derivation of Lemma
3.1
.

We start with the Duhamel identity

e^{-i\tau H}=e^{i\tau\Delta/2}+i{\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{\tau}e^{-i(\tau-s)H}Ve^{is\Delta/2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s.

Iterating this identity
k_{max}
times (later we will set

k_{max}=10^{100}
), we arrive at the expression

e^{-i\tau H}=e^{i\tau\Delta/2}+\sum_{k=1}^{k_{max}}T_{k}(\tau)+R_{k_{max}}(\tau)

where

T_{k}(\tau):=(i{\varepsilon})^{k}\int_{\triangle_{k}(\tau)}e^{-is_{k}\Delta/2}Ve^{-is_{k-1}\Delta/2}V\cdots Ve^{-is_{0}\Delta/2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s_{0}\dots\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s_{k}

and

R_{k_{max}}(\tau):=\int_{0}^{\tau}e^{-i(\tau-s)H}T_{k_{max}}(s)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s,

where
\triangle_{k}(\tau)
is the simplex

\triangle_{k}(\tau):=\{(s_{0},\dots,s_{k})\in({\mathbf{R}}_{+})^{k+1}\mid\sum_{j=0}^{k}s_{j}=\tau\}.

An interpretation of this expansion is that the
first term accounts for the possibility that
the particle moves freely (in a straight line), and the term
T_{k}

accounts for the trajectories a particle might take which involve
k

scattering events. This intuitive picture is only valid for times

\tau\ll{\varepsilon}^{-2}
for which the first term dominates. For longer times,
although the identity still holds, there is significant cancellation
between the terms which precludes any simple interpretation of the terms
on their own.

Our next step is to further decompose the operators
T_{k}
as an integral
over possible phase-space paths of the particle, taking into account both
the spatial localization of the particle and the changes in momentum of
the particle due to scattering. To keep track of the latter we use the
Fourier inversion formula, as expressed in the
identity

\Id=\int\mathinner{|{p}\rangle}\mathinner{\langle{p}|}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}p.

In combination with the following formula for the free evolution of
momentum eigenstates
\mathinner{\langle{x|p}\rangle}=e^{ip\cdot x}

e^{is\Delta/2}\mathinner{|{p}\rangle}=e^{-is|p|^{2}/2}\mathinner{|{p}\rangle},

we obtain the identity

T_{k}(\tau)=(i{\varepsilon})^{k}\int_{\triangle_{k}(\tau)}\int_{({\mathbf{R}}^{d})^{k+1}}\prod_{j=0}^{k}e^{-is_{j}|p_{j}|^{2}/2}\prod_{j=1}^{k}\mathinner{\langle{p_{j}|V|p_{j-1}}\rangle}\mathinner{|{p_{k}}\rangle}\mathinner{\langle{p_{0}}|}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\mathbf{s}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\mathbf{p}}.

Here we have used the boldfaced symbols

{\mathbf{s}}=(s_{0},\dots,s_{k})
and
{\mathbf{p}}=(p_{0},\dots,p_{k})
as a shorthand
for the list of all variables. The next step is to decompose the potential

V
spatially. To do this let
b\in C_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbf{R}}^{d})
be a smooth
positive bump function supported in the unit ball and satisfying
\int b=1
,
and let
b_{r}(x)=r^{-d}b(x/r)
be the scaled version of
b
which preserves
the integral. Then we have the identity

V(x)=\int V(x)b_{r}(y-x)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y.

Now define the localized potential
V_{y}
by

V_{y}(x):=V(x+y)b_{r}(y),

where
b_{r}=r^{-d}b(x/r)
is a scaled version of a bump function

b\in C_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbf{R}}^{d})
that we choose to be supported in
B_{1}
,
be normalized so that
\int b=1
, and satisfy the Gevrey-type Fourier
decay

|{\widehat{b}}(p)|\leq C\exp(-c|p|^{0.999}).

Applying this decomposition into the term
\mathinner{\langle{p|V|q}\rangle}
yields

\mathinner{\langle{p|V|q}\rangle}={\widehat{V}}(p-q)=\int e^{-iy\cdot(p-q)}{\widehat{V_{y}}}(p-q)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y,

and so we write

T_{k}(\tau)=(i{\varepsilon})^{k}\int_{\triangle_{k}(\tau)}\int_{({\mathbf{R}}^{d})^{k+1}}\int_{({\mathbf{R}}^{d})^{k}}\prod_{j=0}^{k}e^{-is_{j}|p_{j}|^{2}/2}\prod_{j=1}^{k}e^{-iy_{j}(p_{j}-p_{j-1})}{\widehat{V_{y_{j}}}}(p_{j}-p_{j-1})\mathinner{|{p_{k}}\rangle}\mathinner{\langle{p_{0}}|}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\mathbf{s}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\mathbf{p}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\mathbf{y}}.

For convenience we introduce the operator

O_{\omega}:=\mathinner{|{p_{k}}\rangle}\mathinner{\langle{p_{0}}|}\prod_{j=0}^{k}e^{-is_{j}|p_{j}|^{2}/2}\prod_{j=1}^{k}e^{-iy_{j}(p_{j}-p_{j-1})}{\widehat{V_{y_{j}}}}(p_{j}-p_{j-1}).

There is an oscillatory phase present in the integral, given by

\displaystyle\varphi_{k}({\mathbf{s}},{\mathbf{p}},{\mathbf{y}})

\displaystyle:=\sum_{j=0}^{k}s_{j}|p_{j}|^{2}/2+\sum_{j=1}^{k}y_{j}\cdot(p_{j}-p_{j-1}).

Aside from this phase, the integrand is smooth to scale
r^{-1}

in the
{\mathbf{p}}
variable, as
V_{y}
is defined to be localized to the
ball of radius
r
.
Thus it makes
sense to compute the stationary points of
\varphi_{k}
with respect
to perturbations in
{\mathbf{p}}
. We observe that, for

0<j<k
,

\displaystyle\partial_{p_{j}}\varphi_{k}=y_{j}+s_{j}p_{j}-y_{j+1}.

We can therefore use stationary phase to localize the integral to sequences
of collision locations
{\mathbf{y}}
which are approximately connected by
the straight line segments
s_{j}p_{j}
. This confirms the picture that

T_{k}
is an integral over paths that the particle can take
in which scattering events are interspersed with free evolution.

We will also need to perform stationary phase in the
{\mathbf{s}}
variable,
but this is delicate due to the fact that we are integrating over the
simplex
\triangle_{k}
, which has sharp corners. Ultimately, integration
by parts in the
{\mathbf{s}}
variables will lead to an approximate conservation
of kinetic energy. There is a slight complication, which is that, due to
the singularity coming from the constraint
s_{j}\geq 0
, the particle may
violate conservation of kinetic energy for short times.

To prove Lemma
3.1
we will first prove a general
oscillatory integral estimate that is adapted for analytic-type phases
and functions. The analyticity is not necessary but simply makes for
convenient estimates.

Lemma 13.1

(General oscillatory integral estimate)
.

Let
\varphi:{\mathbf{R}}^{N}\to{\mathbf{R}}
be an analytic phase satisfying the
estimate

(13.1)

|\partial^{\beta}\varphi({\mathbf{x}})|\leq\prod_{a=1}^{N}(C\ell_{a}(1+\beta_{a}))^{\beta_{a}}.

for any multi-index
\beta
with
|\beta|\geq 2
and some
scales
\{\ell_{a}\}_{a=1}^{N}
.
Also let
w,W:{\mathbf{R}}^{N}\to{\mathbf{R}}
be functions satisfying

(13.2)

|\partial^{\beta}w({\mathbf{x}})|\leq\prod_{a=1}^{N}\ell_{a}^{\beta_{a}}(C(1+\beta_{a}))^{1.001\beta_{a}}W({\mathbf{x}}).

Then

(13.3)

\Big|\int e^{i\varphi({\mathbf{x}})}w({\mathbf{x}})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\mathbf{x}}\Big|\leq C(N)\int W({\mathbf{x}})\prod_{j=1}^{N}\exp(-c_{N}|\ell_{j}^{-1}\partial_{j}\varphi|^{0.99})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\mathbf{x}}.

Proof.

By a rescaling, we can assume that
\ell_{a}=1
for each
a\in[N]
.

Fix a partition of unity

1=b_{0}(x)+\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}b_{j}(x)

where
b_{j}\in C_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbf{R}})
,
b_{0}
is supported on
[-2,2]
, and

b_{j}
is supported on the set
\{2^{j-1}\leq|x|\leq 2^{j+1}\}
, with

(13.4)

\sup_{x}|\partial^{k}b_{j}(x)|\leq 2^{-jk}(Ck)^{1.001k}.

For each
a\in[N]
we therefore have the identity

1=b_{0}(\partial_{a}\varphi)+\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}b_{j}(\partial_{a}\varphi).

We arrive at the decomposition

(13.5)

\int e^{i\varphi({\mathbf{x}})}w({\mathbf{x}})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\mathbf{x}}=\sum_{j_{a}}\int e^{i\varphi({\mathbf{x}})}w({\mathbf{x}})\prod_{a=1}^{N}b_{j_{a}}(\partial_{a}\varphi)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\mathbf{x}}.

The indices
j_{a}
dictate how many times we integrate by parts in
each variable. Specifically, when
j_{a}>0
we integrate by parts

\lfloor j_{a}^{\delta}\rfloor
times, and we let
\alpha({\mathbf{j}})

denote the multi-index corresponding to the indices
{\mathbf{j}}
.
If
j_{a}>0
we integrate by parts in the
a
variable
using the differential operators

(13.6)

\begin{split}L_{a}^{*}f&=-i\frac{1}{\partial_{a}\varphi}\partial_{a}\\
L_{a}f&=i\partial_{a}(\frac{f}{\partial_{a}\varphi}),\end{split}

which satisfy

L_{a}^{*}e^{i\varphi({\mathbf{x}})}=e^{i\varphi({\mathbf{x}})}.

We write
L^{\alpha}
to mean

L_{N}^{\alpha_{N}}\cdots L_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}.

The term corresponding to
{\mathbf{j}}=(j_{1},\dots,j_{N})
, after integration
by parts, is

(13.7)

\begin{split}\int e^{i\varphi({\mathbf{x}})}w({\mathbf{x}})\prod_{a=1}^{N}b_{j_{a}}(\partial_{a}\varphi)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\mathbf{x}}=\int e^{i\varphi({\mathbf{x}})}L^{\alpha({\mathbf{j}})}\Big(w({\mathbf{x}})\prod_{a=1}^{N}b_{j_{a}}(\partial_{a}\varphi)\Big)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\mathbf{x}}\end{split}

Only
C^{N}
terms in the sum (
13.5
) contribute at any
point
{\mathbf{x}}
, so it suffices to prove the bound

(13.8)

L^{\alpha({\mathbf{j}})}\Big(w({\mathbf{x}})\prod_{a=1}^{N}b_{j_{a}}(\partial_{a}\varphi)\Big)\leq C^{N}W(x)\prod_{a=1}^{N}\exp(-c|\partial_{a}\varphi|).

The left hand side here decomposes as a sum of terms of the form

(13.9)

\Big(\prod_{a=1}^{N}(\partial_{a}\varphi)^{-\alpha_{a}-M_{a}}\prod_{k=1}^{m_{a}}\partial^{\beta_{k,a}}\partial_{a}\varphi\Big)\partial^{\gamma}w\prod_{a=1}^{N}\partial^{\nu_{a}}b_{j_{a}}(\partial_{a}\varphi)

where the indices
M_{a}
,
m_{a}
,
\beta_{k,a}
,
\gamma
, and
\nu

satisfy

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{m_{a}}|\beta_{k,a}|

\displaystyle=M_{a}\geq 0

\displaystyle\sum_{a}\sum_{k=1}^{m_{a}}\beta_{k,a}+\gamma+\sum_{j=1}^{N}\nu_{j}

\displaystyle=\alpha({\mathbf{j}}).

Applying (
13.1
), (
13.2
), and (
13.4
), we can
bound (
13.9
) by

(13.10)

\begin{split}\Big(\prod_{a=1}^{N}&(\partial_{a}\varphi)^{-\alpha_{a}-M_{a}}\prod_{k=1}^{m_{a}}\partial^{\beta_{k,a}}\partial_{a}\varphi\Big)\partial^{\gamma}w\prod_{a\in[N]}\partial^{\nu_{a}}b_{j_{a}}(\partial_{a}\varphi)\\
&\leq\Big(\prod_{a=1}^{N}(2^{j_{a}})^{-\alpha_{a}-M_{a}}C(1+M_{a})^{M_{a}}\Big)\prod_{a=1}^{N}(C(1+\gamma_{a}))^{1.001\gamma_{a}}W({\mathbf{x}})\prod_{a=1}^{N}2^{-j_{a}}\prod_{k=1}^{N}(C(1+\nu_{a,k}))^{\nu_{a,k}}\\
&\leq\Big(\prod_{a=1}^{N}2^{-\alpha_{a}j_{a}}(C(1+\alpha_{a}))^{1.001\alpha_{a}}\Big)W({\mathbf{x}}).\end{split}

Because
\partial_{j}\varphi
depends on only
O(1)
variables, each of the
multi-indices
\nu_{a}
and
\beta_{k,a}
are constrained to have only

O(1)
nonzero values (or else the term does not contribute). Thus
there are only

\prod_{a=1}^{N}(C|\alpha_{a}|)^{|\alpha_{a}|}.

possible choices for these indices.

To conclude we show that each such term is bounded by the
right hand side of (
13.8
). Note that only second-order derivatives
of
\varphi
appear in (
13.9
), which is what allows us
to bound
\varphi^{\beta}_{k,j}\partial_{j}\varphi
as well as the derivatives
of
b(\partial_{j}\varphi)
. To see how the
(1+|\partial_{j}\varphi|)^{-K}

term comes about, there are two cases to consider. When
j\in S

this decay comes directly from the
(\partial_{j}\varphi)^{-K-M_{j}}
term
as well as the fact that
|\partial_{j}\varphi|>1
due to the

(1-b(\partial_{j}\varphi))
localization.

On the other hand when
j\not\in S
we have
|\partial_{j}\varphi|\leq 1
,
so
(1+|\partial_{j}\varphi|)^{-K}\geq 2^{-K}
, and so by multiplying

2^{KN}
we can remove such terms from the right hand side.
∎

Lemma 13.2
.

Let
\omega=({\mathbf{s}},{\mathbf{p}},{\mathbf{y}})\in\Omega_{k}(\tau)
be a path,
and let
J
be the index with maximal
s_{J}
. Suppose that
\omega
satisfies

||p_{j}|^{2}/2-|p_{J}|^{2}/2|\leq C\alpha\max\{s_{j}^{-1},|p_{J}|r^{-1}\}.

for each
j\in[0,k]
. Then
\omega
also satisfies

||p_{j}|^{2}/2-|p_{j^{\prime}}|^{2}/2|\leq C^{\prime}\alpha\max\{s_{j}^{-1},s_{j^{\prime}}^{-1},|p_{j}|r^{-1},\alpha r^{-2}\}

for any
j,j^{\prime}\in[0,k]
.

Proof.

Using the triangle inequality we have

||p_{j}|^{2}/2-|p_{j^{\prime}}|^{2}/2|\leq 2C\alpha\max\{s_{j}^{-1},s_{j^{\prime}}^{-1},|p_{J}|r^{-1}\}.

In the case
\max\{s_{j}^{-1},s_{j^{\prime}}^{-1},|p_{J}|r^{-1}\}\geq|p_{J}|r^{-1}
, the conclusion is
trivial. Otherwise, we have in particular
s_{j}^{-1}\leq|p_{J}|r^{-1}
and so

||p_{j}|^{2}/2-|p_{J}|^{2}/2|\leq C\alpha|p_{J}|r^{-1}.

There are two cases to consider now. If
|p_{J}|\gg C\alpha r^{-1}
, then it follows that

||p_{j}|-|p_{J}||{\,\lesssim\,}r^{-1}
, and so in particular
|p_{j}|\gtrsim|p_{J}|
. Otherwise

|p_{J}|r^{-1}\leq\alpha r^{-2}
, and so

\max\{s_{j}^{-1},s_{j^{\prime}}^{-1},|p_{J}|r^{-1}\}{\,\lesssim\,}\max\{s_{j}^{-1},s_{j^{\prime}}^{-1},\alpha r^{-2}\}.

∎

Proof of Lemma
3.1
using Lemma
13.1
.

Using the identity
\Id=\int\mathinner{|{\xi}\rangle}\mathinner{\langle{\xi}|}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi
we have

(13.11)

\begin{split}T_{k}&=\int_{{{\mathbf{R}}^{2d}}}\int_{{{\mathbf{R}}^{2d}}}\mathinner{|{\eta}\rangle}\mathinner{\langle{\xi}|}\mathinner{\langle{\eta|T_{k}|\xi}\rangle}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\eta.\\
&=\int_{{{\mathbf{R}}^{2d}}}\int_{{{\mathbf{R}}^{2d}}}\int_{\Omega_{k}(\tau)}\mathinner{|{\eta}\rangle}\mathinner{\langle{\xi}|}\mathinner{\langle{\eta|O_{\omega}|\xi}\rangle}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\omega\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\eta\end{split}

The idea is to use Lemma
13.1
in the integration over
the
\omega
variables.
As a first step we expand the definition
of the integrand.

(13.12)

\langle\phi_{\eta},O_{\omega}\phi_{\xi}\rangle={\widehat{\phi_{\eta}}}(p_{k})({\widehat{\phi_{\xi}}}(p_{0}))^{*}\prod_{j=0}^{k}e^{-is_{j}|p_{j}|^{2}/2}\prod_{j=1}^{k}e^{-iy_{j}(p_{j}-p_{j-1})}{\widehat{V_{y_{j}}}}(p_{j}-p_{j-1}).

Letting
\xi=(x,p)
and
\eta=(y,q)
, we have

(13.13)

\begin{split}{\widehat{\phi_{\xi}}}(p_{0})&=r^{d/2}e^{-ip_{0}\cdot x}{\widehat{\chi_{env}}}(r(p_{0}-p))\\
{\widehat{\phi_{\eta}}}(p_{k})&=r^{d/2}e^{-ip_{k}\cdot y}{\widehat{\chi_{env}}}(r(p_{k}-q)),\end{split}

where
\chi_{env}
is the envelope function defining the family of
wavepackets, and we can choose this family so that

{\widehat{\chi_{env}}}
has support in the ball
B_{1/r}
.

The integration over
\omega
therefore has an oscillation described
by the phase function

(13.14)

\varphi_{\xi,\eta}(\omega)=p_{0}\cdot x-p_{k}\cdot y-\sum_{j=0}^{k}s_{j}|p_{j}|^{2}/2-\sum_{j=1}^{k}y_{j}\cdot(p_{j}-p_{j-1}).

Before we can apply Lemma
13.1
we need to deal with the
fact that we integrate over
\Omega_{k}(\tau)
, which is singular due to
the integration over the simplex
\triangle_{k}(\tau)
. To deal
with this problem we decompose the indicator function of the simplex
as a sum of smooth functions. The integration over
\triangle_{k}

has the form

\int_{\triangle_{k}(\tau)}f({\mathbf{s}})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\mathbf{s}}=\int_{{\mathbf{R}}^{k+1}}\delta(\tau-\sum s_{j})f({\mathbf{s}})\prod_{j=0}^{k}{\mathbf{1}}_{+}(s_{j})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\mathbf{s}},

where
{\mathbf{1}}_{+}(s)={\mathbf{1}}_{\{s>0\}}
is the indicator function for the
set of positive numbers.

First we decompose
{\mathbf{1}}_{+}
as a sum of smooth functions,

{\mathbf{1}}_{\{s>0\}}=\sum_{j=-\infty}^{\infty}\chi_{j}(s),

where
\rho_{j}
has support
on the set
[2^{j-1},2^{j+1}]
, and each
\rho_{j}
satisfies

\sup|D^{k}\rho_{j}|\leq(C2^{-j}k^{1.001})^{k}.

Given a multi-index
{\mathbf{a}}=(a_{0},a_{1},\dots,a_{k})
, we choose a special index

J=J({\mathbf{a}})
so that
a_{J}
is minimized (breaking ties by, say, choosing the
minimal such index). We take care of the delta function on the
{\mathbf{s}}

variables by writing

s_{J}=\tau-\sum_{j\not=J({\mathbf{a}})}s_{j}.

We will also decompose the integral according to the magnitude of the kinetic
energy
|p_{J}|^{2}/2
. In this case we write

1=\sum_{j=b_{0}}^{\infty}\chi_{j}^{ke}(|p_{J}|^{2}/2),

where
2^{b_{0}}\sim\alpha^{2}r^{-2}
and
\chi_{b_{0}}^{e}
is supported on
[-10r^{-2},10r^{-2}]

and is smooth to scale
r^{-2}
, and each of the
\chi_{j}^{e}
is the same as above
(the letter
ke
simply denotes that these cutoffs are used for kinetic energy).

Finally we can write down the decomposition of the integral that we will use:

(13.15)

\begin{split}\langle\phi_{\eta},T_{k}\phi_{\xi}\rangle=\sum_{{\mathbf{a}}}\sum_{b=b_{0}}^{\infty}&\int_{{\mathbf{R}}^{k+1}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\mathbf{s}}\int_{({\mathbf{R}}^{d})^{k+1}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\mathbf{p}}\int_{({\mathbf{R}}^{d})^{k}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\mathbf{y}}\chi_{a_{J}}(\tau-\sum_{j\not=J}s_{j})\Big(\prod_{j\not=J({\mathbf{a}})}^{k}\chi_{a_{j}}(s_{j})\Big)\chi_{b}^{ke}(|p_{J}|^{2}/2)\\
&\qquad e^{i\varphi({\mathbf{s}},{\mathbf{p}},{\mathbf{y}})}r^{d}{\widehat{\chi_{env}}}(r(p_{0}-p)){\widehat{\chi_{env}}}(r(p_{k}-q))\prod_{j=1}^{k}{\widehat{V_{y_{j}}}}(p_{j}-p_{j-1}).\end{split}

When discussing a particular term in the sum, we will use
E=E(b)=2^{b}
to
denote the ‘dominant’ kinetic energy scale of the path.
Now we compute the differential of
\varphi
with respect to the
\omega

variables (except
s_{J}
, which is determined by the rest of the
s_{j}
)

(13.16)

\begin{split}\partial_{s_{j}}\varphi&=|p_{j}|^{2}/2-|p_{J}|^{2}/2\\
\partial_{p_{j}}\varphi&=y_{j+1}-y_{j}-s_{j}p_{j}.\end{split}

For each
j
let
\ell_{j}:=\alpha\max\{2^{-a_{j}},E^{1/2}r^{-1}\}
, and set

\chi_{main,{\mathbf{a}}}(\omega)=\prod_{j\not=J({\mathbf{a}})}\rho(\ell_{j}^{-1}(|p_{j}|^{2}/2-|p_{J}|^{2}/2))\prod_{j=0}^{k}\rho(\alpha^{-1}r^{-1}(y_{j+1}-y_{j}-s_{j}p_{j})).

The idea is to decompose

\begin{split}\mathinner{\langle{\phi_{\eta},T_{k},\phi_{\xi}}\rangle}=\sum_{{\mathbf{a}}}&\int_{{\mathbf{R}}^{k+1}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\mathbf{s}}\int_{({\mathbf{R}}^{d})^{k+1}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\mathbf{p}}\int_{({\mathbf{R}}^{d})^{k}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\mathbf{y}}(\prod_{j=0;j\not=J({\mathbf{a}})}^{k}\chi_{a_{j}}(s_{j}))\chi_{0}(\tau-\sum_{j\not=J({\mathbf{a}})}s_{j})\\
&\qquad\times e^{i\varphi({\mathbf{s}},{\mathbf{p}},{\mathbf{y}})}r^{d}{\widehat{\chi_{env}}}(r(p_{0}-p)){\widehat{\chi_{env}}}(r(p_{k}-q))\prod_{j=1}^{k}{\widehat{V_{y_{j}}}}(p_{j}-p_{j-1})\\
&\qquad\qquad\times(\chi_{main,{\mathbf{a}}}(\omega)+(1-\chi_{main,{\mathbf{a}}})(\omega)).\end{split}

We will keep the first term (with
\chi_{main,{\mathbf{a}}}
as the main term, and apply
Lemma
13.1
to the second term (with
(1-\chi_{main,{\mathbf{a}}})
) to obtain a bound that is
negligible in the operator norm (after applying Schur’s lemma).

To get the error to be negligible we have to verify that the cutoff function

\chi_{main,{\mathbf{a}}}
is sufficiently smooth. First we investigate the smoothness
of
\chi_{main,{\mathbf{a}}}
in the
p_{j}
variable. The term

\rho(\alpha^{-1}r^{-1}(y_{j+1}-y_{j}-s_{j}p_{j}))

is smooth to scale
\alpha rs_{j}^{-1}
, which is larger than
r
because
r^{2}\geq\tau
. The
other term is

\rho(\ell_{j}^{-1}(|p_{j}|^{2}/2-|p_{J}|^{2}/2)),

which is smooth in the
p_{j}
variable to scale
\ell_{j}|p_{j}|^{-1}
. We claim that

\ell_{j}|p_{j}|^{-1}\gtrsim r
. Rearranging and using our choice of
\ell_{j}
, this is equivalent
to showing

(13.17)

|p_{j}|{\,\lesssim\,}\alpha\max\{2^{-a_{j}}r,E^{1/2}\}

To see this we use the cutoff itself to estimate

||p_{j}|^{2}/2-|p_{J}|^{2}/2|\leq\ell_{j}

so that

||p_{j}|^{2}/2|\leq 2E+\ell_{j}

There are two cases to consider. The first is that

2^{-a_{j}}<E^{1/2}r^{-1}
. In this case,
\ell_{j}=\alpha E^{1/2}r^{-1}
,
and so in particular
|p_{j}|^{2}\leq 2E
, since
E\geq\alpha^{2}r^{-2}
.
Then (
13.17
) immediately follows.

The second case is that
2^{-a_{j}}>E^{1/2}r^{-1}
. In this case,

\ell_{j}=\alpha 2^{-a_{j}}
, so

|p_{j}|\leq\sqrt{2E}+\sqrt{\alpha 2^{-a_{j}}}.

The bound
\sqrt{2E}\leq\frac{\alpha}{2}E^{1/2}
is obvious,
while
\sqrt{\alpha 2^{-a_{j}}}\leq\alpha 2^{-a_{j}}r
follows from

\tau\leq\alpha r^{2}
.

It remains to understand the smoothness of
\chi_{main,{\mathbf{a}},b}
in the

s_{j}
variable. We only need to look at the term

\rho(\alpha^{-1}r^{-1}(y_{j+1}-y_{j}-s_{j}p_{j})),

which is smooth in the
s_{j}
variable to scale
\alpha r|p_{j}|^{-1}
.
We need to show that
\alpha r|p_{j}|^{-1}\geq\ell_{j}^{-1}
in order for the
localization in
\partial_{s_{j}}\varphi
to be effective, and fortunately this is
precisely the inequality we just proved.

Now, when we apply Lemma
13.1
to the error term (with
(1-\chi_{main,{\mathbf{a}},b}(\omega))
),
the integrand is supported on a set with negligible magnitude, and there is no trouble in applying
Schur’s lemma.
∎

14.
A first operator moment estimate

Lemma
3.1
will allow us to
estimate moments of the collision operator
T_{k}
, and in particular
in this section we aim to show that

{\mathbf{E}\,}\|e^{i\sigma H}-e^{-i\sigma\Delta/2}\|_{op}^{m}

with
m\sigma\ll{\varepsilon}^{-2}
.
We will use this with
\sigma={\varepsilon}^{-1.1}

and
m={\varepsilon}^{-0.5}
to show that we can occasionally ‘turn off’ the
potential for a duration
\sigma
. In particular, this allows us to enforce
a constraint such as
s_{0}>\sigma
. Combined with the localization
provided by Lemma
3.1
, this gives a more useful constraint
on the kinetic energy of the path. To see this, consider a path

\omega\in\Omega_{\alpha,k}(t;E)
, and recall the kinetic energy
constraint

||p_{j}|^{2}/2-E|\leq\alpha\max\{E^{1/2}r^{-1},s_{j}^{-1}\}.

If
s_{j}>E^{-1/2}r
(so that the time between the
j
-th and
j+1
-th
collisions is somewhat bigger than
{\varepsilon}^{-1}
), then this implies that

||p_{j}|^{2}/2-E|\leq\alpha E^{1/2}r^{-1},

which in turn is approximately equivalent to

||p_{j}|-(2E)^{1/2}|\leq\alpha r^{-1}.

Notice that this also implies, that for these segments
so that in particular
|x_{j}-x_{j+1}|\gtrsim r
, which is the condition
that the
j
-th and
(j+1)
-th collisions are spatially separated by
the wavepacket scale.
The benefit then of enforcing that
s_{0}\geq\sigma
is that one can replace

E
by
|p_{0}|^{2}/2
.

It is also worth pointing out that the most interesting energy scale
for us is
E\sim 1
. Wavepackets with high energy do not interact
very much with the weak potential, and in
d\geq 3
there are not enough
momenta at low energies to scatter into, and so scattering is also
suppressed in the lower energy regime. So while we keep around the
parameter
E
throughout our discussion of paths, it is safe to imagine
that
E\sim 1
. The following lemma justifies this more formally.

The following lemma states a bound in terms of operator norm. This is
impossible for most stationary potentials because there must exist,
with high probability,
some
region in
{\mathbf{R}}^{d}
on which the
potential behaves badly. Therefore for the next lemma we assume that the
potential is supported in a ball of radius
R={\varepsilon}^{-1000}
(say), so
we work with the potential multiplied by a cutoff,
V\chi_{R}
.

Lemma 14.1
.

There exists a constant
C=C(d)
such that for all
admissible potentials and for
k,m\in{\mathbb{N}}
satisfying
km\leq{\varepsilon}^{-1}
,
the following bound holds:

(14.1)

({\mathbf{E}\,}\|T_{k}(s)\|_{op}^{2m})^{1/{(km)}}\leq R^{C/m}{\varepsilon}^{2}s\,\,(C(km)^{8}|\log{\varepsilon}|^{10}).

Proof.

To estimate the operator norm we will use the bound

\|A\|_{op}^{2m}\leq\trace((A^{*}A)^{m}).

We will compute the trace of the operator

B^{m}:=(T_{k}^{*}T_{k})^{m}
in the wavepacket basis.
We have for any trace-class operator
B
the identity

\trace(B)=\int_{{{\mathbf{R}}^{2d}}}\mathinner{\langle{\xi|B|\xi}\rangle}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi,

which follows from
\trace(B)=\int K_{B}(x,x)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x
(where
K_{B}
is
the Schwartz kernel of
B
). Therefore

(14.2)

{\mathbf{E}\,}\|T_{k}\|_{op}^{2m}\leq\int_{{{\mathbf{R}}^{2d}}}{\mathbf{E}\,}\mathinner{\langle{\xi|((T_{k}^{\chi})^{*}T_{k}^{\chi})^{m}|\xi}\rangle}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi.

We expand each of the operators
T_{k}^{\chi}
as an integral over
paths
\omega_{\ell}

(14.3)

\Gamma=(\xi_{0},\omega_{1},\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\omega_{2},\xi_{3},\xi_{4},\cdots,\xi_{4m-2},\omega_{2m},\xi_{4m-1})

be a sequence of paths with intermediate points in phase space, and write

(14.4)

\begin{split}{\mathbf{E}\,}\mathinner{\langle{\xi|((T_{k}^{\chi})^{*}T_{k}^{\chi})^{m}|\xi}\rangle}=\int\mathinner{\langle{\xi|\xi_{4m-1}}\rangle}\mathinner{\langle{\xi_{0}|\xi}\rangle}\prod_{\ell=1}^{2m-1}\mathinner{\langle{\xi_{2\ell-1}|\xi_{2\ell}}\rangle}\prod_{\ell=1}^{2m}\chi_{\alpha}(\omega_{\ell};\xi_{2\ell-2},\xi_{2\ell-1}){\mathbf{E}\,}\prod_{\ell=1}^{2m}\mathinner{\langle{\xi_{2\ell-1}|O_{\omega_{\ell}}|\xi_{2\ell}}\rangle}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\bm{\omega}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\bm{\xi}.\end{split}

We expand the integrand above using the identity

\mathinner{\langle{\xi_{2\ell-2}|O_{\omega_{\ell}}|\xi_{2\ell-1}}\rangle}={\varepsilon}^{k}\mathinner{\langle{\xi_{2\ell-2}|p_{\ell,0}}\rangle}\mathinner{\langle{p_{\ell,k}|\xi_{2\ell-1}}\rangle}e^{i\varphi(\omega_{\ell})}\prod_{j=1}^{k}{\widehat{V_{y_{\ell,k}}}}(q_{\ell,k}).

Let
K=[1,2m]\times[1,k]
be the indexing set for the collisions, and
let
P(\Gamma)\in{\mathcal{P}}(K)
be the partition of
K
induced by the path

\Gamma
(
P(\Gamma)
is the finest partition of
K
such that
a\sim_{P}b

when
|a-b|\leq 2r
). Then using
|\mathinner{\langle{\xi|p}\rangle}|\leq Cr^{d/2}
and
Lemma
B.3
we can bound the expectation in the integrand
as follows:

\big|{\mathbf{E}\,}\prod_{\ell=1}^{2m}\mathinner{\langle{\xi_{2\ell-1}|O_{\omega_{\ell}}|\xi_{2\ell}}\rangle}\big|\leq(Cr)^{dmk}{\varepsilon}^{2mk}\prod_{a\in K}(1+|q_{a}|)^{-20d}{\mathbf{1}}(|y_{a}|\leq R)\sum_{Q\leq P(\Gamma)}\prod_{S\in Q}|S|^{2|S|}r^{-d}\exp(-c|\frac{r}{|S|}\sum_{a\in S}q_{a}|^{0.99}).

The term
{\mathbf{1}}(|y_{a}|\leq R)
comes from the fact that we are actually
working with the localized potential
V\chi_{R}
on a ball of radius
R={\varepsilon}^{-100}
.

The combinatorial factor
\prod_{S\in Q}|S|^{2|S|}
is at most
(2km)^{4km}
,
and the sum over
Q\leq P(\Gamma)
involves at most
|{\mathcal{P}}(K)|\leq(Ckm)^{2km}
terms.

To prove Lemma
14.1
it therefore suffices to
prove that for any
Q,P\in{\mathcal{P}}(K)
with
Q\leq P
the bound

(14.5)

\begin{split}\int&r^{dmk}|\langle{\xi|\xi_{4m-1}}\rangle||\langle{\xi_{0}|\xi}\rangle|\prod_{\ell=1}^{2m-1}|\langle{\xi_{2\ell-1}|\xi_{2\ell}}\rangle\prod_{\ell=1}^{2m}\chi_{\alpha}(\omega_{\ell};\xi_{2\ell-2},\xi_{2\ell-1}){\mathbf{1}}(P(\Gamma)=P)\\
&\qquad\qquad\prod_{a\in K}(1+|q_{a}|)^{-20d}{\mathbf{1}}(|y_{a}|\leq R)\prod_{S\in Q}r^{-d}\exp(-c|\frac{r}{|S|}\sum_{a\in S}q_{a}|^{0.99})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\Gamma\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\leq(Cmk)^{2mk}({\varepsilon}^{2}t)^{km}(1+R^{-C}d_{r}(\xi,(0,0)))^{-2d}.\end{split}

First we will prove the bound without the localization
(1+R^{-C}d_{r}(\xi,\bm{0}))^{-2d}
,
and then explain how to modify the argument to obtain this (very mild) localization,
which is simply required to have integrability in
\xi
.

The proof of (
14.5
) follows very closely the argument in the proof of
Proposition
4.10
. That is, we order the variables
in the path
\Gamma
and define constraints on each
variable
p_{a}
,
y_{a}
, and
\xi_{\ell}
in terms of variables that are earlier in the ordering.

The variable labels are given by the set

\Lambda=\{({\mlq\mlq s\mrq\mrq},a),({\mlq\mlq p\mrq\mrq},a),({\mlq\mlq y\mrq\mrq},a)\}_{a\in K}\cup\{({\mlq\mlq\xi\mrq\mrq},\ell)\}_{\ell=0}^{4m-1}.

We order the variables so that
({\mlq\mlq y\mrq\mrq},q)\leq{\mlq\mlq p\mrq\mrq},a)\leq({\mlq\mlq s\mrq\mrq},a)
and

({\mlq\mlq\xi\mrq\mrq},2\ell-2)\leq({\mlq\mlq y\mrq\mrq},\ell,j)\leq({\mlq\mlq\xi\mrq\mrq},2\ell-1)
, and otherwise
so that the ordering is consistent with the usual ordering on
K
and
[0,4m-1]
.
We use thise ordering to define the partial paths
\Gamma_{\leq\lambda}
and

\Gamma_{<\lambda}
for
\lambda\in\Lambda
.

In the path integral we are considering there is no global conservation of kinetic energy because
we do not have any lower bounds on
s_{\ell,0}
or
s_{\ell,k}
. Given a path
\Gamma
,
we define a local kinetic energy
E_{\ell}
to be the value of
|p_{\ell,j}|^{2}/2
with

j=\argmax s_{\ell,j}
. Now we can define the local variable constraints:

(14.6)

f_{{\mlq\mlq p\mrq\mrq},a}(\bm{\Gamma}_{\leq{\mlq\mlq p\mrq\mrq},a}):=\begin{cases}r^{d}\exp(-c|\frac{r}{km}\sum_{a\in S}q_{a}|^{0.99}),&a=\max S\text{ for some }S\in Q\\
r^{d}{\mathbf{1}}(|p_{\ell,0}-(\xi_{2\ell-2})_{p}|\leq\alpha r^{-1}),&a=(\ell,0)\\
(1+|p_{a}-p_{a-1}|)^{-20d}{\mathbf{1}}(||p_{a}|-E_{\ell}||\leq\alpha\max\{|E_{\ell}|^{-1/2}s_{a}^{-1},r^{-1}\}),&\text{ else}.\end{cases}

The standard
y
constraints are given by

(14.7)

f_{{\mlq\mlq y\mrq\mrq},a}(\bm{\Gamma}_{\leq{\mlq\mlq y\mrq\mrq},a}):=\begin{cases}r^{-d}{\mathbf{1}}(|y_{\ell,1}-((\xi_{2\ell-2})_{x}+s_{\ell,0}p_{\ell,0})|\leq\alpha r),&a=(\ell,1)\\
r^{-d}{\mathbf{1}}(|y_{\ell,j}-(y_{\ell,j-1}+s_{\ell,j-1}p_{\ell,j-1})|\leq\alpha r),&a=(\ell,j)\text{ for }j>1.\end{cases}

Finally, the standard
\xi
constraints are

(14.8)

f_{{\mlq\mlq\xi\mrq\mrq},\ell}(\bm{\Gamma}_{\leq{\mlq\mlq\xi\mrq\mrq},\ell}):=\begin{cases}|\mathinner{\langle{\xi|\xi_{0}}\rangle}|&\ell=0\\
|\mathinner{\langle{\xi_{2n-1}|\xi_{2n}}\rangle}|,&\ell=2n,n\in[1,2m-1]\\
{\mathbf{1}}(d_{r}(\xi_{2n-1},(y_{n,k}+s_{n,k}p_{n,k},p_{n,k}))\leq\alpha),&\ell=2n-1,n\in[1,2m].\end{cases}

A simple accounting of terms (unpacking the constraints
present in
\chi_{\alpha}(\omega_{\ell};\xi_{2\ell-2},\xi_{2\ell-1})
confirms the estimate

(14.9)

\begin{split}&r^{dmk}|\langle{\xi|\xi_{4m-1}}\rangle||\langle{\xi_{0}|\xi}\rangle|\prod_{\ell=1}^{2m-1}|\langle{\xi_{2\ell-1}|\xi_{2\ell}}\rangle\prod_{\ell=1}^{2m}\chi_{\alpha}(\omega_{\ell};\xi_{2\ell-2},\xi_{2\ell-1})\\
&\qquad\qquad\prod_{a\in K}(1+|q_{a}|)^{-20d}{\mathbf{1}}(|y_{a}|\leq R)\prod_{S\in Q}r^{-d}\exp(-c|\frac{r}{|S|}\sum_{a\in S}q_{a}|^{0.99})\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\leq\prod_{a\in K}f_{{\mlq\mlq p\mrq\mrq},a}(\Gamma_{\leq{\mlq\mlq p\mrq\mrq},a})f_{{\mlq\mlq y\mrq\mrq},a}(\Gamma_{\leq{\mlq\mlq p\mrq\mrq},a})\prod_{\ell=0}^{4m-1}f_{{\mlq\mlq\xi\mrq\mrq},\ell}(\Gamma_{\leq{\mlq\mlq\xi\mrq\mrq},\ell}).\end{split}

Now for the time variable constraints
f_{{\mlq\mlq s\mrq\mrq},a}
we use
the indicator function
{\mathbf{1}}(P(\Gamma)=P)
as follows:

(14.10)

f_{{\mlq\mlq s\mrq\mrq},a}(\Gamma_{\leq{\mlq\mlq s\mrq\mrq},a}):=\begin{cases}{\mathbf{1}}_{[0,t]}(s_{a}),&a=\min S\text{ for some }S\in Q\\
{\mathbf{1}}(|y_{\min S}-(y_{a-1}+s_{a-1}p_{a-1})|\leq 4\alpha r),&a\in S\setminus\{\min S\}\text{ for some }S\in Q.\end{cases}

For
a=(\ell,j)\in S\setminus\{\min S\}
, we have the estimate

\sup_{\Gamma_{<{\mlq\mlq s\mrq\mrq},a}}\int f_{{\mlq\mlq s\mrq\mrq},a}(\Gamma_{\leq{\mlq\mlq s\mrq\mrq},a})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s_{a}\leq 4E_{\ell}^{-1/2}\alpha r.

For such
a
, the integral over
p_{a-1}
produces a factor
of
\alpha r^{-1}\min\{E_{\ell}^{(d-1)/2},1\}
, so multiplying these
factors yields at a contribution of at most
C\alpha^{2}
,
since
\min\{E^{(d-2)/2},E^{-1}\}\leq 1
for any
E
.

Therefore, multiplying the factors from all of the variables together,
and using Lemma
4.19
to deal with immediate
recollisions, we arrive at the estimate

\begin{split}\int&r^{dmk}|\langle{\xi|\xi_{4m-1}}\rangle||\langle{\xi_{0}|\xi}\rangle|\prod_{\ell=1}^{2m-1}|\langle{\xi_{2\ell-1}|\xi_{2\ell}}\rangle\prod_{\ell=1}^{2m}\chi_{\alpha}(\omega_{\ell};\xi_{2\ell-2},\xi_{2\ell-1}){\mathbf{1}}(P(\Gamma)=P)\\
&\qquad\qquad\prod_{a\in K}(1+|q_{a}|)^{-20d}{\mathbf{1}}(|y_{a}|\leq R)\prod_{S\in Q}r^{-d}\exp(-c|\frac{r}{|S|}\sum_{a\in S}q_{a}|^{0.99})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\Gamma\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\leq(C\alpha^{10}mk)^{2mk}{\varepsilon}^{2km}t^{|Q|},\end{split}

and since
Q
has at most
mk
clusters,
{\varepsilon}^{2km}t^{|Q|}\leq({\varepsilon}^{2}t)^{km}
.

The localization term
(1+R^{-C}d_{r}(\xi,\bm{0}))^{-2d}
comes from the following additional
considerations. First, if
|\xi_{x}|\geq 10\alpha R|\xi_{p}|+10R
, then any choice of

s_{1,0}
and
p_{1,0}
forces
|y_{1,1}|>R
, and therefore the integrand vanishes.
If
|\xi_{p}|<R^{2}
then this enforces an upper bound on
d_{r}(\xi,\bm{0})\leq R^{C}
.
On the other hand if
|\xi_{p}|>R^{2}
then either
|p_{a}|\geq{\varepsilon}^{-10}R
for all
a\in K
,
in which case each time variable is constrained to be at most
{\varepsilon}^{10}
so that the collision
locations do not escape the ball
B_{R}
. Otherwise if
|p_{a}|\leq{\varepsilon}^{-10}R
for some
a
,
then
\prod(1+|q_{a}|)^{-20d}\leq R^{-10d}|\xi_{p}|^{-10d}
and we still obtain decay in terms
of
d_{r}(\xi,\bm{0})
.
∎

15.
Interspersing the free evolution
