The primary application of Lemma
14.1

is to show that, up to a small error, we may intersperse the evolution
under the Hamiltonian
H
with periods of evolution under the free
Hamiltonian
-\Delta/2
. This corresponds to evolution of a system in
which the potential is periodically turned off and on again in a smooth way.

This modification is convenient for ensuring conservation of kinetic
energy across path segments. One way to see how this is helpful is the
following physical interpretation. The channel
{\mathcal{E}}_{\tau}^{N}
corresponds
to an evolution in which the potential discontinuously changes at
every time
j\tau
,
j\in[N]
. The abrupt time-dependence of the potential
disturbs conservation of energy and in particular conservation of kinetic
energy. We restore an approximate conservation of kinetic energy by smoothly
ramping up and down the strength of the potential.

Lemma 15.1
.

Define the unitary operator
U(s)
by

U(s):=e^{i(\tau-s)H}e^{-is\Delta/2}.

Then, for
\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{m}<{\varepsilon}^{-1.5}
,
and sufficiently small
{\varepsilon}
, we have

{\mathbf{E}\,}\|e^{im\tau H}-U(\sigma_{m})U(\sigma_{m-1})\cdots U(\sigma_{1})\|_{op}^{2}\leq m^{2}{\varepsilon}^{2/13}.

To prove Lemma
15.1
we first need a bound on

\|e^{-i\tau H}-e^{i\tau\Delta/2}\|
. Here we use the
Duhamel expansion

e^{-i\sigma H}-e^{i\sigma\Delta/2}=\sum_{k=1}^{k_{max}}T_{k}(\sigma)+R_{k_{max}}(\sigma).

To estimate the remainder
R_{k}
we will use the bound

\|R_{k}(\sigma)\|_{op}\leq\sigma\sup_{s<\sigma}\|T_{k}(s)\|_{op},

which follows from the triangle inequality and the unitarity of

e^{-i(\sigma-s)H}
.

Using these ingredients, we can compare the random evolution to the
free evolution.

Lemma 15.2
.

For
\sigma<{\varepsilon}^{-1.5}
and
m<{\varepsilon}^{-1/30}
,
and sufficiently small
{\varepsilon}>0
,
we have

{\mathbf{E}\,}\|e^{i\sigma H}-e^{-i\sigma\Delta/2}\|^{m}\leq{\varepsilon}^{m/8}.

Proof.

The Duhamel formula gives the exact decomposition

(15.1)

e^{isH}=e^{-is\Delta/2}+\sum_{1\leq k\leq k_{max}}T_{k}(s)+R_{k}(s).

Applying the triangle inequality and using unitarity in the definition
of
R_{k}
, we have the bound

(15.2)

\|e^{isH}-e^{-is\Delta/2}\|\leq\sum_{1\leq k\leq k_{max}}\|T_{k}(s)\|+\int_{0}^{s}\|T_{k_{max}}(s^{\prime})\|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s^{\prime}.

Applying the inequality
(\sum_{i=1}^{k}a_{i})^{p}\leq k^{p}\sum a_{i}^{p}

for
a_{i}\geq 0
and
p>0
, we have

\|e^{isH}-e^{-is\Delta/2}\|^{m}\leq(k_{max}+1)^{m}\sum_{1\leq k\leq k_{max}}\|T_{k}(s)\|^{m}+(k_{max}+1)^{m}\Big(\int_{0}^{s}\|T_{k_{max}}(s^{\prime})\|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s^{\prime}\Big)^{m}.

Using Holder’s inequality on the integral and then taking expectations
with Lemma
14.1
, we have

(15.3)

\begin{split}{\mathbf{E}\,}\|e^{isH}-e^{-is\Delta/2}\|^{m}&\leq C^{m}\sum_{1\leq k\leq k_{max}}({\mathbf{E}\,}\|T_{k}(s)\|^{m})+C^{m}s^{m-1}\int_{0}^{s}{\mathbf{E}\,}\|T_{k_{max}}(s^{\prime})\|^{m}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s^{\prime}\\
&{\,\lesssim\,}C^{m}\sum_{1\leq k\leq k_{max}}({\mathbf{E}\,}\|T_{k}(s)\|^{2Mm})^{1/2M}+R^{C}({\varepsilon}^{2}s^{1+1/k_{max}}((k_{max}m)^{8}|\log{\varepsilon}|^{10})^{km})^{k_{max}m}\\
&{\,\lesssim\,}C^{m}\sum_{1\leq k\leq k_{max}}R^{C/M}({\varepsilon}^{2}s(kMm)^{8}|\log{\varepsilon}|^{10})^{mk/2}\\
&\qquad\qquad+R^{C}({\varepsilon}^{2}s^{1+1/k_{max}}((k_{max}m)^{8}|\log{\varepsilon}|^{10})^{km})^{k_{max}m}\end{split}

By choosing
M
to be a sufficiently large constant, we can have
the bound
R^{C/M}\leq{\varepsilon}^{-10^{-5}}
(say). Then, since

m\leq{\varepsilon}^{-1/30}
and
s\leq{\varepsilon}^{-1.5}
,

{\varepsilon}^{2}sm^{8}\leq{\varepsilon}^{2}s{\varepsilon}^{-1/10}\leq{\varepsilon}^{1/4}.

Therefore the sum over
k
converges geometrically so
that the main contribution comes from
k=1
, which
gives

R^{C/M}({\varepsilon}^{2}sm^{8}|\log{\varepsilon}|^{10})^{m/2}{\,\lesssim\,}{\varepsilon}^{m/8}
.
∎

We are now ready to prove Lemma
15.1
.

Proof of Lemma
15.1
.

With
\bm{\sigma}=(\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{m})
, let

U(\bm{\sigma}):=U(\sigma_{m})U(\sigma_{m-1})\cdots U(\sigma_{1}),

and let
\bm{\sigma}^{(k)}
be the sequence obtained by replacing
the first
k
elements of
\bm{\sigma}
by zeros. That is,

\bm{\sigma}^{(k)}:=(0,\cdots,0,\sigma_{k+1},\cdots,\sigma_{m}).

Then we use the triangle inequality,

\|U(\bm{\sigma}^{(m)})-U(\bm{\sigma}^{(0)})\|_{op}\leq\sum_{k=1}^{m}\|U(\bm{\sigma}^{(k)})-U(\bm{\sigma})^{(k-1)})\|_{op}.

We square both sides and use Cauchy-Schwartz to find

\|U(\bm{\sigma}^{(m)})-U(\bm{\sigma}^{(0)})\|_{op}^{2}\leq m\sum_{k=1}^{m}\|U(\bm{\sigma}^{(k)})-U(\bm{\sigma})^{(k-1)})\|_{op}^{2}.

By unitarity, we can simplify the terms on the right hand side to

{\mathbf{E}\,}\|U(\bm{\sigma}^{(m)})-U(\bm{\sigma}^{(0)})\|_{op}^{2}\leq m\sum_{k=1}^{m}{\mathbf{E}\,}\|e^{i\sigma_{k}H}-e^{-i\sigma_{k}\Delta/2}\|_{op}^{2}\leq m^{2}{\varepsilon}^{2/13}.

∎

The reason we are doing this is that we want to replace the short time
evolution
e^{i\tau H}
by an evolution operator that allows for a small
amount of free evolution at the beginning – this is convenient both
for using conservation of kinetic energy as discussed at the top of this
section, and to guarantee some amount of decorrelation between adjacent
path segments.

Let
S={\varepsilon}^{-1.5}
and let

\rho\in C_{c}^{\infty}([\sigma,2\sigma])
be a smooth Gevrey function satisfying

\int\rho=1
.
2
2

2

The Gevrey regularity is for convenience, but

C^{m}
for some large enough
m
depending on
k_{max}
is fine.

Let
U_{\rho}
be the operator

U_{\rho}(\tau):=\int e^{i(\tau-s)H}e^{-is\Delta/2}\rho(s)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s.

By averaging
Lemma
15.1
, we have

{\mathbf{E}\,}\|e^{iN\tau}-U_{\rho}(\tau)^{N}\|_{op}\leq N{\varepsilon}^{1/13}.

Moreover,
U_{\rho}(\tau)
has a Duhamel expansion of the form

U_{\rho}(\tau)=e^{-i\tau\Delta/2}+\sum_{k=1}^{k_{max}}T_{k,\rho}(\tau)+R_{k_{max},\rho}(\tau),

where

T_{k,\rho}(\tau):=\int_{\Omega_{k}(\tau)}P(s_{0})O_{\omega}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\omega,

and

(15.4)

P(s)=\int_{0}^{s}\rho(s^{\prime})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s^{\prime}.

The benefit of doing this is that
we can therefore enforce
s_{0}>{\varepsilon}^{-1.5}>r
in every Duhamel expansion,
and this more easily guarantees that the kinetic energy level will be
maintained in every path segment. To notate this, we define

\Omega_{\alpha,k}(t,E,S):=\{({\mathbf{s}},{\mathbf{p}},{\mathbf{y}})\in\Omega_{\alpha,k}(t,E)\mid s_{0}>S\}.

Note that, for such paths, if
E^{1/2}r^{-1}>S
,

||p_{0}|^{2}/2-E|\leq\alpha E^{1/2}r^{-1}
, so we have for each
j\in[k]
the
bound

||p_{j}|^{2}/2-|p_{0}|^{2}/2|\leq 2\alpha\max\{E^{1/2}r^{-1},s_{j}^{-1}\}.

Thus, except at very low frequencies (
E{\,\lesssim\,}{\varepsilon}^{0.5}
, so

|p_{0}|{\,\lesssim\,}{\varepsilon}^{0.25}
), we can replace
E
by
|p_{0}|^{2}/2
.

Appendix A
The Boltzmann limit for short times

In this section we compare the
random Schrodinger evolution on short time scales
s\ll{\varepsilon}^{-2}

to the Boltzmann evolution given by the equation

(A.1)

\partial_{t}a(x,p)+p\cdot\nabla_{x}a(x,p)={\varepsilon}^{2}\int\delta(|p|^{2}-|q|^{2})R(p-q)(a(x,q)-a(x,p))\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}q.

To compare the quantum evolution channel
{\mathcal{E}}_{s}
defined in (
1.7
)
and the linear Boltzmann equation (
1.8
), we use a perturbative
expansion for each equation and compare each term. Because we are working
with such short time scales (compared to the kinetic time
{\varepsilon}^{-2}
),
we only need to use a few terms in the expansion.

A.1.
The finite Duhamel expansion

Indeed we have as a consequence of Lemma
14.1
that just two
terms of the Duhamel expansion suffices to approximate the Schrodinger evolution.

Lemma A.1
.

For times
s<{\varepsilon}^{-1.5}
and any
\delta>0
there exists
C_{\delta}
such that

{\mathbf{E}\,}\|e^{-isH}-(T_{0}(s)+T_{1}(s)+T_{2}(s))\|_{op}^{2}\leq C_{\delta}{\varepsilon}^{-\delta}({\varepsilon}^{2}s)^{3}.

Proof.

We follow the proof of Lemma
15.2
.
We beign with the exact Duhamel expansion

(A.2)

e^{isH}=\sum_{k\leq 2}T_{k}(s)+\sum_{2<k\leq k_{max}}T_{k}(s)+R_{k_{max}}(s).

Applying the triangle inequality and using unitarity in the definition
of
R_{k}
, we have the bound

(A.3)

\|e^{isH}-\sum_{k\leq 2}T_{k}(s)\|_{op}\leq\sum_{2<k\leq k_{max}}\|T_{k}(s)\|_{op}+\int_{0}^{s}\|T_{k_{max}}(s^{\prime})\|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s^{\prime}.

Squaring both sides and using the Cauchy-Schwartz inequality, we have

\|e^{isH}-e^{-is\Delta/2}\|^{2}\leq(k_{max}+1)^{2}\sum_{1\leq k\leq k_{max}}\|T_{k}(s)\|^{2}+(k_{max}+1)^{2}s\int_{0}^{s}\|T_{k_{max}}(s^{\prime})\|^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s^{\prime}.

Then taking expectations using Lemma
14.1

we have

(A.4)

\begin{split}{\mathbf{E}\,}\|e^{isH}-e^{-is\Delta/2}\|^{2}&\lesssim\sum_{1\leq k\leq k_{max}}({\mathbf{E}\,}\|T_{k}(s)\|^{4M})^{1/2M}+s\int_{0}^{s}{\mathbf{E}\,}\|T_{k_{max}}(s^{\prime})\|^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s^{\prime}\\
&{\,\lesssim\,}C^{m}\sum_{1\leq k\leq k_{max}}R^{C/M}(C^{M}{\varepsilon}^{2}s|\log{\varepsilon}|^{10})^{k}\\
&\qquad\qquad+R^{C}(C{\varepsilon}^{2}s^{1+1/k_{max}}|\log{\varepsilon}|^{10})^{k_{max}}\end{split}

By choosing
M
to be a sufficiently large constant, we can have
the bound
R^{C/M}\leq{\varepsilon}^{-\delta}
. The main term
is the term coming from
T_{3}(s)
which gives the bound above.
∎

Using Lemma
A.1
and the operator Cauchy-Schwartz
inequality we have for
s\leq{\varepsilon}^{-1.5}
that

(A.5)

\|{\mathcal{E}}_{s}[A]-{\mathbf{E}\,}(\sum_{k=0}^{2}T_{k}(s))^{*}A(\sum_{k=0}^{2}T_{k}(s))\|_{op}\leq C({\varepsilon}^{1.99}s)^{3}\|A\|_{op}.

Defining the superoperator

{\mathcal{T}}_{k,k^{\prime}}(s)[A]={\mathbf{E}\,}T_{k}(s)^{*}AT_{k^{\prime}}(s),

the approximation (
A.5
) can be written

{\mathcal{E}}_{s}\approx{\mathcal{T}}_{0,0}+{\mathcal{T}}_{1,0}+{\mathcal{T}}_{0,1}+{\mathcal{T}}_{1,1}+{\mathcal{T}}_{2,0}+{\mathcal{T}}_{0,2}+{\mathcal{T}}_{2,1}+{\mathcal{T}}_{1,2}+{\mathcal{T}}_{2,2}.

The terms
{\mathcal{T}}_{0,1}
and
{\mathcal{T}}_{1,0}
vanish identically
because
{\mathbf{E}\,}V=0
. Moreover the higher order terms satisfy

\|{\mathcal{T}}_{2,1}[A]+{\mathcal{T}}_{1,2}[A]+{\mathcal{T}}_{2,2}[A]\|_{op}\leq C({\varepsilon}^{3}s+{\varepsilon}^{4}s^{2})\|{\mathcal{W}_{A}}\|_{C^{2d+1}}.

The factor
{\varepsilon}^{3}s
comes from
{\mathcal{T}}_{2,1}[A]+{\mathcal{T}}_{1,2}[A]
,
in which there are three collisions (and therefore
{\varepsilon}^{3}
) which must
form a single cluster (hence the single factor of
s
). The term

{\mathcal{T}}_{2,2}[A]
contributes
{\varepsilon}^{4}s^{2}
because there may be
two clusters of two collisions each that may happen at any pair of times.

We therefore have the approximation

(A.6)

{\mathcal{E}}_{s}\approx{\mathcal{T}}_{0}(\sigma)+{\mathcal{T}}_{1,1}+{\mathcal{T}}_{0,2}+{\mathcal{T}}_{2,0}

where, expanding the definitions we can write

(A.7)

\begin{split}{\mathcal{T}}_{0}[A]&=e^{-i\sigma\Delta/2}Ae^{i\sigma\Delta/2}\\
{\mathcal{T}}_{1,1}[A]&:={\mathbf{E}\,}\int_{0}^{\sigma}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s\int_{0}^{\sigma}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s^{\prime}\,\,e^{-i(\sigma-s^{\prime})\Delta/2}Ve^{-is^{\prime}\Delta/2}Ae^{is\Delta/2}Ve^{i(\sigma-s)\Delta/2}\\
{\mathcal{T}}_{0,2}[A]&:={\mathbf{E}\,}\int_{0}^{\sigma}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s\int_{0}^{\sigma-s}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\tau\,\,e^{-i\sigma\Delta/2}Ae^{is\Delta/2}Ve^{i\tau\Delta/2}Ve^{i(\sigma-s-\tau)\Delta/2}\\
{\mathcal{T}}_{2,0}[A]&:={\mathbf{E}\,}\int_{0}^{\sigma}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s\int_{0}^{\sigma-s}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\tau\,\,e^{-i(\sigma-s-\tau)\Delta/2}Ve^{-i\tau\Delta/2}Ve^{-is\Delta/2}Ae^{i\sigma\Delta/2}.\end{split}

The following lemma makes this approximation precise.

Lemma A.2
.

Let
A\in{\mathcal{H}}(L^{2}({\mathbf{R}}^{d}))
be a bounded operator and let
s\leq{\varepsilon}^{-1.5}
. Then

(A.8)

\|{\mathcal{E}}_{s}[A]-({\mathcal{T}}_{0}(s)+{\mathcal{T}}_{1,1}(s)+{\mathcal{T}}_{0,2}(s)+{\mathcal{T}}_{2,0}(s))[A]\|_{op}\leq{\varepsilon}^{2.5}s\|{\mathcal{W}_{A}}\|_{C^{2d+1}}.

A.2.
Comparing to the linear Boltzmann equation

We recall below the statement of Proposition
2.1
that
See
2.1

The first step is to write out the action of the superoperators
{\mathcal{T}}
on the Wigner
transforms of the operator.
An elementary computation using the formula

{\mathbf{E}\,}{\widehat{V}}(q){\widehat{V}}^{*}(q^{\prime})=\delta(q-q^{\prime}){\widehat{R}}(q),

where
R(x)={\mathbf{E}\,}V(0)V(x)
yields the following identities:

(A.9)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{W}_{{\mathcal{T}}_{0}(\sigma)A}}(x,p)

\displaystyle={\mathcal{W}_{A}}(x+\sigma p,p)

(A.10)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{W}_{{\mathcal{T}}_{1,1}A}}(x,p)

\displaystyle={\varepsilon}^{2}\int_{0}^{\sigma}\int_{0}^{\sigma}\int_{{\mathbf{R}}^{d}}e^{i(s^{\prime}-s)(|p^{\prime}|^{2}-|p|^{2})/2}R(p-p^{\prime})

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad{\mathcal{W}_{A}}(x+(\sigma-\frac{s+s^{\prime}}{2})p+\frac{s+s^{\prime}}{2}p^{\prime},p^{\prime})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}p^{\prime}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s^{\prime}

(A.11)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{W}_{{\mathcal{T}}_{0,2}A+{\mathcal{T}}_{2,0}A}}(x,p)

\displaystyle=2{\varepsilon}^{2}\Rept\int_{0}^{\sigma}\int_{0}^{\sigma-S}\int_{{\mathbf{R}}^{d}}e^{-iu(|p^{\prime}|^{2}-|p|^{2})/2}R(p^{\prime}-p)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad{\mathcal{W}_{A}}(x-(\sigma-u/2)p+up^{\prime}/2,p)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}p^{\prime}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}S\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}u.

The classical solution has an analogous expansion in terms of the Duhamel formula,

a_{s}\approx T_{0}(s)a_{0}+T_{1}(s)a_{0}+T_{2}(s)a_{0}=:T_{\leq 2}(s)a_{0}.

where

(A.12)

\displaystyle T_{0}(t)a(x,p)

\displaystyle=a(x+tp,p)

(A.13)

\displaystyle T_{1}(t)a(x,p)

\displaystyle={\varepsilon}^{2}\int_{0}^{t}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s\int\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}p^{\prime}\delta(|p^{\prime}|^{2}-|p|^{2})R(p-p^{\prime})a(x+(t-s)p+sp^{\prime},p^{\prime})

\displaystyle={\varepsilon}^{2}\int_{0}^{t}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s\int\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}p^{\prime}\delta(|p^{\prime}|^{2}-|p|^{2})R(p-p^{\prime})a(x+sp+(t-s)p^{\prime},p^{\prime})

(A.14)

\displaystyle T_{2}(t)a(x,p)

\displaystyle=-{\varepsilon}^{2}tK(p)a(x+tp,p)

where the scattering cross section
K(p)
is given by

K(p)=\int\delta(|p^{\prime}|^{2}-|p|^{2})R(p-p^{\prime})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}p^{\prime}.

We will prove the following approximation result for the classical evolution.

Lemma A.3
.

Let
a_{0}\in C^{2d+1}
, and suppose that
a_{s}
solves the linear Boltzmann
equation (
A.1
), and suppose that
s\leq{\varepsilon}^{-1.5}
. Then

\|a_{s}-T_{\leq 2}(s)a_{0}\|_{C^{2d+1}}\leq C{\varepsilon}^{3}s\|a_{0}\|_{C^{2d+1}}.

We will show that
{\mathcal{W}_{{\mathcal{T}}_{\leq 2}[A]}}
is close to
T_{\leq 2}{\mathcal{W}_{A}}
by comparing
the three terms to each other one at a time.
The first terms agree identically,

(A.15)

{\mathcal{W}_{{\mathcal{T}}_{0}(\sigma)A}}=T_{0}({\mathcal{W}_{A}}),

so there are two terms remaining, which we separate into two lemmas.

Lemma A.4
.

Let
a\in C^{2d+1}({{\mathbf{R}}^{2d}})
be an observable supported
on
\{(x,p)||p|>{\varepsilon}^{0.2}\}
and let
{\varepsilon}^{-1.2}\leq s\leq{\varepsilon}^{-1.5}
.
Then

(A.16)

\|{\mathcal{W}_{{\mathcal{T}}_{1,1}(s)[\Op(a)]}}-T_{1}(s)a\|_{C^{2d+1}_{r}}\leq{\varepsilon}^{2}s\delta\|a\|_{C^{4d+2}_{r,\delta}}.

Lemma A.5
.

Let
a\in C^{2d+1}({{\mathbf{R}}^{2d}})
be an observable supported
on
\{(x,p)||p|>{\varepsilon}^{0.2}\}
and let
{\varepsilon}^{-1.2}\leq s\leq{\varepsilon}^{-1.5}
.
Then

(A.17)

\|{\mathcal{W}_{({\mathcal{T}}_{0,2}+{\mathcal{T}}_{2,0})(s)[\Op(a)]}}-T_{2}(s)a\|_{C^{2d+1}}\leq{\varepsilon}^{2}\delta s\|a\|_{C^{4d+2}_{r,\delta}}.

Before we prove these lemmas we stop to verify that the lemmas imply the main result.

Proof of Proposition
2.1
.

Combining Lemma
A.2
, Lemma
A.3
, and
the bound
\|\Op(a)\|_{op}\leq\|a\|_{C^{2d+1}_{r}}
, it suffices to prove that

\|{\mathcal{W}_{{\mathcal{T}}_{\leq 2}(s)[\Op(a)]}}-T_{\leq 2}(s)a\|_{C^{2d+1}_{r}}\leq{\varepsilon}^{?}\|a\|_{C^{2d+1}_{r}}.

This bound then follows from the identity (
A.15
) and the
bounds (
A.16
) and (
A.17
).
∎

A.3.
The proof of Lemma
A.4

Proof of Lemma
A.4
.

We recall the formula (
A.10
) for
{\mathcal{T}}_{1,1}[\Op(a)]

and introduce the convenient change of variables
S=(s+s^{\prime})/2
and
u=s-s^{\prime}
to write

\displaystyle{\mathcal{W}_{{\mathcal{T}}_{1,1}[\Op(a)]}}(x,p)

\displaystyle={\varepsilon}^{2}\int_{0}^{\sigma}\int_{0}^{\sigma}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s^{\prime}\int_{{\mathbf{R}}^{d}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}p\,\,\,\,e^{i(s^{\prime}-s)(|p^{\prime}|^{2}-|p|^{2})/2}R(p-p^{\prime}){\mathcal{W}_{A}}(x+(\sigma-\frac{s+s^{\prime}}{2})p+\frac{s+s^{\prime}}{2}p^{\prime},p^{\prime})

\displaystyle={\varepsilon}^{2}\iint\int_{{\mathbf{R}}^{d}}{\mathbf{1}}_{[0,\sigma]}(S-u/2){\mathbf{1}}_{[0,\sigma]}(S+u/2)e^{iu(|p^{\prime}|^{2}-|p|^{2})/2}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad R(p-p^{\prime}){\mathcal{W}_{A}}(x+(\sigma-S)p+Sp^{\prime},p^{\prime})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}p^{\prime}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}S\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}u.

One key difference between the integrand (
A.10
) defining

{\mathcal{W}_{{\mathcal{T}}_{1,1}[\Op(a)]}}(x,p)
and
the integrand in (
A.13
) defining
T_{1}a

is that (
A.13
)
contains a delta function
\delta(|p|^{2}-|p^{\prime}|^{2})
,
whereas (
A.10
)
involves a phase
e^{i(s^{\prime}-s)(|p^{\prime}|^{2}-|p|^{2})}

and an additional time variable. If we could integrate
out the
s^{\prime}-s
variable we would reproduce this delta function exactly, but there
is a hard cutoff from
0\leq s,s^{\prime}\leq\sigma
which ruins the sharp decay off
of the annulus
|p|^{2}=|p^{\prime}|^{2}
.

We therefore decompose the indicator function of the interval as the sum of
a smooth piece containing the bulk of the interval and a sharp piece at either end,

(A.18)

{\mathbf{1}}_{[0,\sigma]}(s;p)=\chi_{[0,\sigma]}(s,p)+h(s,p),

where we take
\chi_{[0,\sigma]}(s,p)
to be smooth to scale
r|p|^{-1}
in the variable
s
,
and
h(s,p)
to be supported on the set

\supp h(\cdot,p)\subset\begin{cases}[0,r|p|^{-1}]\cup[\sigma-r|p|^{-1},\sigma],&|p|\geq\frac{1}{2}{\varepsilon}^{0.2}\\
[0,r{\varepsilon}^{0.2}]\cup[\sigma-r{\varepsilon}^{0.2},\sigma],&|p|<\frac{1}{2}{\varepsilon}^{0.2}.\end{cases}

The function
h(s,p)
can be chosen to be smooth to scale
{\varepsilon}^{0.2}
in the momentum variable
p

(for example by constructing it using a partition of unity on dyadic annuli, the thinnest of which has
scale
{\varepsilon}^{0.1}
).
We use the decomposition (
A.18
) in both the
s
and
s^{\prime}
variables
to write

{\mathcal{W}_{{\mathcal{T}}_{1,1}[\Op(a)]}}=Ma+Ea,

where
Ma
is the main term

(A.19)

\begin{split}Ma(x,p)&:={\varepsilon}^{2}\iint\int_{{\mathbf{R}}^{d}}\chi_{[0,\sigma]}(S+u/2,p)\chi_{[0,\sigma]}(S-u/2,p)e^{iu(|p^{\prime}|^{2}-|p|^{2})/2}R(p-p^{\prime})\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad a(x+(\sigma-S)p+Sp^{\prime},p^{\prime})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}u\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}S\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}p^{\prime}.\end{split}

and
Ea
is the error term

\displaystyle Ea(x,p):={\varepsilon}^{2}\iint\int_{{\mathbf{R}}^{d}}

\displaystyle(h(S-u/2;p){\mathbf{1}}_{[0,\sigma]}(S+u/2)+\chi(S-u/2;p)h(S+u/2,p))

\displaystyle\qquad e^{iu(|p^{\prime}|^{2}-|p|^{2})/2}R(p-p^{\prime})a(x+(\sigma-S)p+Sp^{\prime},p^{\prime})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}u\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}S\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}p^{\prime}.

Our next step is to demonstrate the bound

(A.20)

\|Ea\|_{C^{2d+1}_{r}}\leq\|a\|_{C^{4d+2}_{r}}.

To exploit the cancellation in the phase, we will integrate
by parts in the
u
variable. To do this we first split the integration in
p^{\prime}
into a part for which the kinetic
energy is approximately conserved and a part for which the conservation of kinetic energy is significantly violated,

1={\widetilde{{\mathbf{1}}}}(|p|^{2}-|p^{\prime}|^{2}{\,\lesssim\,}r^{-1}|p|)+{\widetilde{{\mathbf{1}}}}(|p|^{2}-|p^{\prime}|^{2}\gtrsim r^{-1}|p|),

where the function
{\widetilde{{\mathbf{1}}}}
is meant to indicate a smoothed indicator function.
This allows us to decompose the error term into a term with
p^{\prime}
localized to the annulus and second more oscillatory
term,

Ea=E^{KE}a+E^{osc}a,

with

\displaystyle E^{KE}a(x,p):={\varepsilon}^{2}\iint\int_{{\mathbf{R}}^{d}}

\displaystyle(h(S-u/2;p){\mathbf{1}}_{[0,\sigma]}(S+u/2)+\chi(S-u/2;p)h(S+u/2,p)){\widetilde{{\mathbf{1}}}}(|p|^{2}-|p^{\prime}|^{2}{\,\lesssim\,}r^{-1}|p|)

\displaystyle\qquad e^{iu(|p^{\prime}|^{2}-|p|^{2})/2}R(p-p^{\prime})a(x+(\sigma-S)p+Sp^{\prime},p^{\prime})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}u\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}S\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}p^{\prime}.

To bound
E^{KE}a
in the
C^{2d+1}_{r}
norm we have to first observe that all terms in
the integrand are bounded in
C^{2d+1}_{r}
except for the oscillation
e^{iu(|p^{\prime}|^{2}-|p|^{2})}

which has gradient

\nabla_{p}e^{iu(|p^{\prime}|^{2}-|p|^{2})/2}=pue^{iu(|p^{\prime}|^{2}-|p|^{2})/2}.

To handle this term, we can integrate by parts in the
p^{\prime}
variable, and since the rest of the integrand
is smoooth in
p^{\prime}
to scale
r^{-1}
we can obtain decay in the
u
variable for
|u|\gg r|p|^{-1}
.
We integrate by parts
2d+1
times (again splitting the integral into a piece with

|u|{\,\lesssim\,}r|p|^{-1}
and a piece with
|u|\gg r|p|^{-1}
, and only integrating by parts in the
second piece). We choose
2d+1
because we need to establish differentiability to order
2d+1
in the
p

variable, and each derivative brings down a factor of
u
. This comes at a cost of an additional
2d+1

derivatives of the function
a
.
Now we can use the triangle inequality to bound the norm:
the integration over
s,s^{\prime},p
is over a set of
volume
(r^{2}|p|^{-1})^{2}\times(r^{-1}|p|^{d-1})
(the first factor coming from the integration in time, the
second from the integration over the annulus in
p
). The volume of integration is therefore at most

r|p|^{d-2}
, which is further localized in the case
|p|\gg 1
by the presence of the term
{\widehat{R}}(p-p^{\prime})
,
so that at the end we obtain a bound of the form

\|E^{KE}a\|_{C^{2d+1}_{r}}{\,\lesssim\,}{\varepsilon}^{2}r|p|^{-1}\min\{|p|^{d-2},1\}\|a\|_{C^{2d+1}_{r}}{\,\lesssim\,}{\varepsilon}^{0.8}\|a\|_{C^{4d+2}_{r}}.

For the second term
E^{osc}a
supported away from this annulus,
we integrate by parts in the
u
variable. This produces several boundary terms
E^{bd}_{j}a
and several
bulk terms
E^{bulk}_{j}a
.
One of the boundary terms (which we arbitrarily number
E^{bd}_{1}a

comes from the delta function
\delta(S-u/2)
in the derivative of

h(S-u/2)
with respect to
u
,

E^{bd}_{1}a(x,p):={\varepsilon}^{2}\int\int_{{\mathbf{R}}^{d}}\frac{e^{iS/2(|p^{\prime}|^{2}-|p|^{2})}}{|p|^{\prime 2}-|p|^{2}}{\mathbf{1}}(|p|^{2}-|p^{\prime}|^{2}\gtrsim r^{-1}|p|){\widehat{R}}(p-p^{\prime})a(x+(\sigma-u/2)p+up^{\prime}/2,p^{\prime})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}u\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}p^{\prime}.

This time, as
S{\,\lesssim\,}r|p|^{-1}
even the oscillatory term is sufficiently smooth in the
p
variable
(and this holds for all of the boundary terms). We can therefore use the triangle inequality once
more, this time being careful to account for a logarithm coming from te weak localization to the annulus
(but converging due to the
{\widehat{R}}
localization) to obtain

\|E^{bd}a\|_{C^{2d+1}_{r}}\leq{\varepsilon}^{0.8}|\log{\varepsilon}|\|a\|_{C^{2d+1}_{r}}.

The remaining terms to consider are the bulk terms. These come from the smooth
part of the derivative of
h(s,p)
. One of these bulk terms is given by

\displaystyle E^{blk}_{1}(x,p)

\displaystyle:={\varepsilon}^{2}\iint\int_{{\mathbf{R}}^{d}}h^{\prime}(S-u/2,p){\mathbf{1}}_{[0,\sigma]}(S+u/2)\frac{e^{iu(|p^{\prime}|^{2}-|p|^{2})/2}}{|p|^{2}-|p^{\prime}|^{2}}R(p-p^{\prime})

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad a(x+(\sigma-S)p+Sp^{\prime},p^{\prime})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}u\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}S\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}p^{\prime},

To bound this term we integrate by parts once more in the
u
variable.
This produces more boundary terms
E^{blk,bd}_{j}
that are controlled using the argument above, in addition
to one more bulk term having improved decay off of the annulus, so for example one of the terms is

\displaystyle E^{blk,blk}_{1}a(x,p)

\displaystyle:={\varepsilon}^{2}\iint\int_{{\mathbf{R}}^{d}}h^{\prime\prime}(S-u/2,p){\mathbf{1}}_{[0,\sigma]}(S+u/2)\frac{e^{iu(|p^{\prime}|^{2}-|p|^{2})/2}}{(|p|^{2}-|p^{\prime}|^{2})^{2}}R(p-p^{\prime})

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad a(x+(\sigma-S)p+Sp^{\prime},p^{\prime})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}u\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}S\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}p^{\prime}.

This term is bounded using the same argument as
E^{KE}a

since now the localization to the annulus is strong enough
that the triangle inequality can again be used. This concludes our proof of (
A.20
).

Now proceed with the calculation of the main term
Ma(x,p)
, which we recall below

\displaystyle Ma(x,p)={\varepsilon}^{2}\int

\displaystyle\chi_{[0,\sigma]}(S-u/2,p)\chi_{[0,\sigma}(S+u/2,p)e^{iu(|p^{\prime}|^{2}-|p|^{2})/2}

\displaystyle\qquad{\widehat{R}}(p-p^{\prime})a(x+(\sigma-S)p+S^{\prime}p,p^{\prime})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}p^{\prime}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}S\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}u.

A main character in this calculation is the function

(A.21)

g_{p,\sigma}(S,\beta)=\int\chi_{[0,\sigma]}(S-u/2,p)\chi_{[0,\sigma}(S+u/2,p)e^{iu\beta}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}u.

By integrating by parts in the
u
variable, we can see that
this function decays rapidly when

|\beta|\gtrsim|p|r^{-1}
.
Using the coarea formula in the form

(A.22)

\int_{{\mathbf{R}}^{d}}f(p^{\prime})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}p^{\prime}=\int_{\mathbf{R}}\int_{\{|p|^{2}-|p^{\prime}|^{2}=\beta\}}\frac{1}{2|p^{\prime}|}f(p^{\prime})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\mathcal{H}}^{n-1}(p^{\prime})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\beta,

we have the identity

Ma(x,p)={\varepsilon}^{2}\int_{[0,\sigma]}\int_{{\mathbf{R}}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\beta g_{p,\sigma}(S,\beta)\int_{\{(|p|^{2}-|p^{\prime}|^{2})/2=\beta\}}\frac{1}{|p^{\prime}|}R(p-p^{\prime})a(x+(\sigma-S)p+Sp^{\prime},p^{\prime})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\mathcal{H}}^{n-1}(p^{\prime})

By the Fourier inversion formula,

\int g_{p,\sigma}(S,\beta)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\beta=\chi_{[0,\sigma]}(S,p)^{2}.

The idea is that the integration in
\beta
will therefore recover the term

\chi_{p,\sigma}(S)^{2}
, so that
Ma(x,p)
can be compared to the function

T^{M}_{1}a(x,p):={\varepsilon}^{2}\int_{0}^{t}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s\int\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}p^{\prime}\delta(|p^{\prime}|^{2}-|p|^{2})\chi_{[0,\sigma]}(S,p)^{2}R(p-p^{\prime})a(x+sp+(t-s)p^{\prime},p^{\prime}),

which we will later show is close to
T_{1}a
.
We will change variables to move the integration over
the sphere
|p^{\prime}|^{2}/2=|p|^{2}/2+\beta
to integration over the sphere
|p^{\prime}|=|p|

in order to better compare the terms. To this end we choose
\kappa(p,\beta)

satisfying

|(1+\kappa(p,\beta))p|^{2}=|p|^{2}+\beta.

The function
\kappa(p,\beta)
has the explicit formula

\kappa(p,\beta)=\sqrt{1+\frac{\beta}{|p|^{2}}}-1.

For
|p|>100r^{-1}
(which holds except when
\beta\gg{\varepsilon}^{0.2}
, which is
suppressed due to the decay of
g
), we have the bound

\kappa(\beta,p)\leq C\frac{\beta}{|p|^{2}}.

Then applying the change of variables
p^{\prime}=(1+\kappa)q
, we can write

(A.23)

\begin{split}Ma(x,p)={\varepsilon}^{2}\int_{0}^{\sigma}&\int\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\beta g_{p,\sigma}(S,\beta)\int_{\{|q|=|p|\}}\frac{(1+\kappa)^{d-2}}{|p|}\\
&R(p-(1+\kappa)q)a(x+(\sigma-S)p+S(1+\kappa)q,(1+\kappa)q)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\mathcal{H}}^{n-1}(q).\end{split}

The classical counterpart
T^{M}_{1}a
can be written

T^{M}_{1}a(x,p)={\varepsilon}^{2}\int_{0}^{\sigma}\int\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\beta g_{p,\sigma}(S,\beta)\int_{\{|q|=|p|\}}\frac{1}{|p|}R(p-q)a(x+(\sigma-S)p+Sq,q)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\mathcal{H}}^{n-1}(q).

To compare these integrals we define the function

H(x,p;\kappa,S,q):=|p|^{-1}(1+\kappa)^{d-2}R(p-(1+\kappa)q)a(x+(\sigma-S)p+S(1+\kappa)q,(1+\kappa)q).

Then

(A.24)

Ma(x,p)-T^{M}_{1}a(x,p)={\varepsilon}^{2}\int_{0}^{\sigma}\int g(S,\beta)\big(\int_{\{|q|=|p|\}}\int_{0}^{\kappa(p,\beta)}\partial_{\kappa}H(x,p;\kappa,S,q)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\kappa\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\mathcal{H}}^{n-1}(q)\big)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\beta\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}S

And

(A.25)

\begin{split}\partial_{\kappa}H(x,p;\kappa,S,q)&=\frac{(d-2)(1+\kappa)^{d-3}}{|p|}R(p-(1+\kappa)q)a(x+(\sigma-S)p+S(1+\kappa)q,(1+\kappa)q)\\
&+\frac{(1+\kappa)^{d-2}}{|p|}q\cdot\nabla R(p-(1+\kappa)q)a(x+(\sigma-S)p+S(1+\kappa)q,(1+\kappa)q)\\
&+\frac{(1+\kappa)^{d-2}}{|p|}R(p-(1+\kappa)q)q\cdot\nabla_{2}a(x+(\sigma-S)p+S(1+\kappa)q,(1+\kappa)q)\\
&+\frac{(1+\kappa)^{d-2}}{|p|}R(p-(1+\kappa)q)Sq\cdot\nabla_{1}a(x+(\sigma-S)p+S(1+\kappa)q,(1+\kappa)q).\end{split}

Of these terms the most substantial are the latter two terms in which the gradient hits
a
.
Using the scaling of the derivatives in
C^{k}_{r}
and the fact that
S\ll r^{2}
we obtain the bound

\|\partial_{\kappa}H\|_{C^{k}_{r}}\leq r(1+|p|^{-1})\|a\|_{C^{k+1}_{r}}.

However to prove Lemma
A.4
we need to assume more than microscopic smoothness on

a
. Assuming more smoothness on
a
, we have the bound

\|\partial_{\kappa}H\|_{C^{k}_{r}}\leq r\delta(1+|p|^{-1})\|a\|_{C^{k+1}_{r,\delta}}.

Then applying the triangle inequality to (
A.24
) we obtain the bound

(A.26)

\begin{split}\|Ma-T^{M}_{1}a\|_{C^{2d+1}_{r}}&\leq C{\varepsilon}^{2}\sigma\delta\|a\|_{C^{2d+2}_{r,\delta}}.\end{split}

To conclude the proof we need to estimate
\|T^{M}_{1}a-T_{1}a\|_{C^{2d+1}_{r}}
. This function is given
by the integral

T^{M}_{1}a-T_{1}a=\int_{0}^{\sigma}(1-\chi_{[0,\sigma]}(S,p)^{2})\int\delta(|p^{\prime}|^{2}-|p|^{2})R(p-p^{\prime})a(x+sp+(\sigma-s)p^{\prime},p^{\prime})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}p^{\prime}

To obtain bounds on the momentum derivatives we treat the radial derivative
p\cdot\nabla_{p}

separately from tangential derivatives
\vec{v}\cdot\nabla_{p}
where
\vec{v}(p)\cdot p=0
is a tangential
vector field. For radial derivatives we integrate by parts in the
p^{\prime}
variable using the identity

p\cdot\nabla p\delta(|p^{\prime}|^{2}-|p|^{2})=-p^{\prime}\cdot\nabla_{p^{\prime}}\delta(|p^{\prime}|^{2}-|p|^{2}).

The tangential derivatives on the other hand vanish on the delta function. Combining this argument with a
triangle inequality in the integral over
s
and the fact that
1-\chi_{[0,\sigma]}^{2}
is supported
on
[0,r|p|^{-1}]\cup[\sigma-r|p|^{-1},\sigma]
and
|p|\geq{\varepsilon}^{0.2}
, we conclude

\|T^{M}_{1}a-T_{1}a\|_{C^{2d+1}_{r}}{\,\lesssim\,}{\varepsilon}^{0.8}\|a\|_{C^{2d+1}_{r}}.

∎

Proof of Lemma
A.5
.

The proof of Lemma
A.5
is very similar to the proof of
Lemma
A.4
, and for this reason we abbreviate the argument.
Using

2\Rept e^{iu(|p^{\prime}|^{2}-|p|^{2})}=e^{iu(|p^{\prime}|^{2}-|p|^{2})}+e^{-iu(|p^{\prime}|^{2}-|p|^{2})}

to symmetrize the domain of integration over
u
, we can rewrite (
A.11
)
as

{\mathcal{W}_{({\mathcal{T}}_{0,2}+{\mathcal{T}}_{2,0})[\Op(a)]}}(x,p)={\varepsilon}^{2}\int_{0}^{\sigma}\int_{-(\sigma-S)}^{\sigma-S}\int_{{\mathbf{R}}^{d}}e^{iu(|p^{\prime}|^{2}-|p|^{2})/2}R(p^{\prime}-p)a(x-(\sigma-u/2)p+up^{\prime}/2,p)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}p^{\prime}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}u\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}S.

Before directly comparing this function to
T_{2}a
, we compare it to the intermediate
quantity

{\widetilde{T}}_{2}a(x,p):={\varepsilon}^{2}\int_{0}^{\sigma}\int_{-(\sigma-S)}^{\sigma-S}\int_{{\mathbf{R}}^{d}}e^{iu(|p^{\prime}|^{2}-|p|^{2})/2}R(p^{\prime}-p)a(x-\sigma p,p)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}p^{\prime}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}u\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}S.

The function

e(x,p;u,p^{\prime}):=a(x-\sigma p,p)-a(x-\sigma p+u(p-p^{\prime})/2,p)

satisfies

\|e\|_{C^{2d+1}_{r}}{\,\lesssim\,}\delta(r^{-1}|up^{\prime}|+u|p|)\|a\|_{C^{2d+2}_{r,\delta}}.

We use the smoothness of
a
to scale
r^{-1}
in
p
to reduce the domain of integration
to
|up^{\prime}|{\,\lesssim\,}r
.

Again we would like to integrate by parts in the
u
variable to obtain localization of
p^{\prime}
to
the annulus. Following the proof of Lemma
A.4
, we decompose
the indicator function
{\mathbf{1}}_{[-(\sigma-S),\sigma-S]}
as the sum of a piece that is smooth
to scale
r|p|^{-1}
and a piece that is supported on two intervals of that width. The bounds for
the error pieces follow exactly as in the previous proof. What remains is a main term of the form

m(x,p)={\varepsilon}^{2}\int_{0}^{\sigma}\chi(S;p)\int_{-(\sigma-S)}^{(\sigma-S)}\chi_{S}(u;p)\int_{{\mathbf{R}}^{d}}e^{iu(|p^{\prime}|^{2}-|p|^{2})}R(p^{\prime}-p)a(x-(\sigma-u/2)p+up^{\prime}/2,p)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}p^{\prime}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}u\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}S.

We define the function

g(\beta;S,\sigma,p):=\int\chi_{S}(u;p)e^{iu\beta}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}u,

and observe that the smoothness of
\chi_{S}
enforces that
g(\beta;S,\sigma,p)
decays rapidly
for
\beta\geq r^{-1}|p|
. Moreover, by the Fourier inversion formula,

\int g(\beta;S,\sigma,p)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\beta=1.

Then using the coarea formula as in (
A.22
) we have

m(x,p)={\varepsilon}^{2}\int_{0}^{\sigma}\chi(S;p)\int\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\beta g(\beta;S,\sigma,p)\int_{(|p|^{2}-|p^{\prime}|^{2})/2=\beta}|p^{\prime}|^{-1}R(p-p^{\prime})a(x+(\sigma-u/2)p+Sp^{\prime},p^{\prime})

∎

Appendix B
Using graphs, forests, and partitions to compute moments

In this section we introduce an abstract lemma about graphs that is
useful in a variety of contexts within this paper. The simplest
context is in the computation of the moments

{\mathbf{E}\,}\prod_{j=1}^{k}{\widehat{V_{y_{j}}}}(q_{j})=\int e^{-iq_{j}\cdot x_{j}}\big({\mathbf{E}\,}\prod_{j=1}^{k}V(x_{j})\big)b_{r}(x_{j}-y_{j})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\mathbf{x}}.

In the integral above, we can split the expectation into smaller
pieces depending on the configuration of
{\mathbf{x}}
. More precisely,
let
G({\mathbf{x}})
be the graph on index set
[1,k]
with edges
(i,j)

when
|x_{i}-x_{j}|\leq 1
. Letting
P({\mathbf{x}})\in{\mathcal{P}}([k])
be the
partition of
[k]
into the connected components of
G({\mathbf{x}})
,
we have

{\mathbf{E}\,}\prod_{j=1}^{k}V(x_{j})=\prod_{S\in P({\mathbf{x}})}{\mathbf{E}\,}\prod_{j\in S}V(x_{j}).

One way to perform the desired splitting is to write, for each pair

i<j
,

1=(1-{\mathbf{1}}(|x_{i}-x_{j}|\leq 1))+{\mathbf{1}}(|x_{i}-x_{j}|\leq 1).

Taking a product over all
i
and
j
produces the identity

(B.1)

\begin{split}1&=\prod_{i<j}((1-{\mathbf{1}}(|x_{i}-x_{j}|\leq 1))+{\mathbf{1}}(|x_{i}-x_{j}|\leq 1))\\
&=\sum_{G}\prod_{(i,j)\in G}{\mathbf{1}}(|x_{i}-x_{j}|\leq 1)\prod_{(i,j)\not\in G}(1-{\mathbf{1}}(|x_{i}-x_{j}|\leq 1)),\end{split}

where the sum is over all graphs on vertex set
[k]
.
The problem with this approach is that the number of graphs scales like

2^{\Theta(n^{2})}
, whereas we can typically only afford factors on
the order
2^{\Theta(n\log n)}
. Since we only need enough information
about
G({\mathbf{x}})
to determine
P({\mathbf{x}})
(of which there are at most

n^{n}
possibilities), this seems like a wasteful sum.

The solution is to work with a compressed representation of
G({\mathbf{x}})

that only records its minimal spanning forest. For
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
let
\binom{[n]}{2}
be the set of possible edges
\{(i,j)\mid 1\leq i<j\leq n\}

on a graph with vertex set
[n]
. Let
w:\binom{[n]}{2}\to{\mathbf{R}}_{+}
be
any function that assigns each edge a unique positive weight.
Given a graph
G
, let
P(G)\in{\mathcal{P}}([n])
be the partition of
[n]

into the connected components of
G
.
Then, given a graph
G\subset\binom{[n]}{2}
, the minimal spanning
forest
F_{G}
of
G
is defined to be the unique graph having the same connected
components
(P(G)=P(F_{G}))
and minimizing the sum
\sum_{e\in F}w(e)
.

The graph
F_{G}
is acyclic, as otherwise edges can be trimmed to reduce
the total weight of the graph. A formula due to Borchardt but attributed
to Cayley states that the number of trees on a graph of
n
vertices is
exactly
n^{n-2}
. Any acylic graph on
n
vertices can be obtained by
forming a tree on
n+1
vertices and deleting a vertex. Therefore

N_{F}(n)\leq(n+1)^{n-1}\leq Cn^{n}.

This is closer to the number of partitions on
n
vertices, so there is
not as much wasted information.

We will start with the identity

(B.2)

1=\sum_{F}{\mathbf{1}}(F_{G}=F),

which trivially holds for any graph
G
, where the sum is over all acyclic graphs
F
.
The good thing about this sum is that it is a sum over only
O(n^{n})
terms, and

F_{G}
determines
P(G)
, so the decomposition gives enough information to split the
expectation.

We set
{\mathcal{G}}(F)
to be the collection of graphs

{\mathcal{G}}(F)=\{G\subset\binom{[n]}{2}\mid F_{G}=F\}.

We will express the set
{\mathcal{G}}(F)
using sets that depend on more local information
about the graph. In particular, given a set
S\subset[n]
of vertices and a graph
G
,
we write
G|_{S}
for the induced graph on
S
. A “local approximation” to
{\mathcal{G}}(F)

is the collection
{\mathcal{L}}(F)
,

{\mathcal{L}}(F_{0})=\{G\subset\binom{[n]}{2}\mid F_{G|_{S}}=F_{0}|_{S}\text{ for all }S\in P(F_{0})\}.

We note that
{\mathcal{L}}(F)
has an alternative definition. Given a forest
F
and a weight
w
,
define the weight

w_{F}(e):=\begin{cases}w(e),&P(F\cup\{e\})=P(F)\\
w(e)+\max_{e}w,&P(F\cup\{e\})>P(F).\end{cases}

That is,
w_{F}
adds extra weight to each edge between distinct connected components of
F
.
Let
F(G;w_{F})
be the minimal forest of the graph
G
with the modified weight
w_{F}
. Then

{\mathcal{L}}(F_{0})=\{G\subset\binom{[n]}{2}\mid F(G;w_{F_{0}})\supset F_{0}\}.

This motivates the definition of the collection

{\mathcal{G}}(F^{\prime};F_{0}):=\{G\subset\binom{[n]}{2}\mid F(G;w_{F_{0}})=F^{\prime}\}.

With this definition, it is clear that we have the identity

{\mathbf{1}}_{{\mathcal{L}}(F_{0})}=\sum_{F^{\prime}\supset F_{0}}{\mathbf{1}}_{{\mathcal{G}}(F^{\prime};F_{0})}.

We can now apply Mobius inversion in the partially ordered set
{\mathcal{F}}
of forests, ordered
by inclusion. Note that
{\mathcal{F}}
is an ideal in the poset of graphs, so the Mobius function
is the same. The Mobius inversion formula, along with the identity

{\mathcal{G}}(F_{0};F_{0})={\mathcal{G}}(F_{0})
, yields

(B.3)

{\mathbf{1}}_{{\mathcal{G}}(F_{0})}=\sum_{F^{\prime}\supset F_{0}}(-1)^{|F^{\prime}\setminus F_{0}|}{\mathbf{1}}_{{\mathcal{L}}(F^{\prime})}.

We conclude this discussion by deriving polynomial identities from the
decompositions (
B.2
) and (
B.3
).
Given a forest
F_{0}
, let
D(F_{0})
be the set of edges “dominated” by
F
,

D(F_{0}):=\{e\in\binom{[n]}{2}\mid F_{F_{0}\cup\{e\}}=F_{0}\}.

That is,
D(F_{0})
consists of those edges that are already connected by
F_{0}
along
a path consisting of edges
e^{\prime}
with
w(e^{\prime})<w(e)
. Another important collection of edges
are those “connected” by
F_{0}
,

C(F_{0}):=\{e\in\binom{[n]}{2}\mid P(F_{0}\cup\{e\})=P(F_{0})\}.

Then we can express the indicator functions
{\mathbf{1}}_{{\mathcal{G}}(F)}
and
{\mathbf{1}}_{{\mathcal{L}}(F)}

as follows:

{\mathbf{1}}_{{\mathcal{G}}(F)}=\prod_{e\in F}{\mathbf{1}}(e\in G)\prod_{e\not\in D(F)}(1-{\mathbf{1}}(e\in G))

and

{\mathbf{1}}_{{\mathcal{L}}(F)}=\prod_{e\in F}{\mathbf{1}}(e\in G)\prod_{e\in C(F)\setminus D(F)}(1-{\mathbf{1}}(e\in G)).

Therefore, by (
B.2
), we have that for any
\{0,1\}
-valued variables

Y_{e}
,

(B.4)

1=\sum_{F}\prod_{e\in F}Y_{e}\prod_{e\not\in D(F)}(1-Y_{e}).

The right hand side is a multilinear polynomial that agrees with
1
on the hypercube
\{0,1\}^{N}
, so it follows
that in fact (
B.4
) holds for any variables
Y_{e}
. Similarly, we have
that

(B.5)

\prod_{e\in F}Y_{e}\prod_{e\not\in D(F)}(1-Y_{e})=\sum_{F^{\prime}\supset F}(-1)^{|F^{\prime}\setminus F|}\prod_{e\in F^{\prime}}Y_{e}\prod_{e\in C(F)\setminus D(F)}(1-Y_{e}).

holds for any values
Y_{e}
and any forest
F
.

B.1.
Application to computing moments

As a useful example, we can now estimate the moments of the Fourier transform of a localized potential,

{\mathbf{E}\,}\prod_{j=1}^{k}{\widehat{V_{y_{j}}}}(q_{j}).

Definition B.1

(Localized potentials)
.

For
y\in{\mathbf{R}}^{d}
, define
V_{y}
to be the function

V_{y}(x)=b_{r}(x)V(x-y),

where
b_{r}
is a function satisfying
\int b_{r}(x)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x=1
,

\supp b_{r}\subset B_{10r}
, and

|{\widehat{b_{r}}}(p)|\leq C\exp(-c(r|p|)^{0.999})

Definition B.2

(Admissible potentials)
.

A random potential
V
is
admissible
if it is stationary,
mean-zero, has range-
1
dependence, and satisfies the following moment bound

(B.6)

\sup_{{\mathbf{x}}}\Big|{\mathbf{E}\,}\prod_{j=1}^{k}\partial^{\alpha_{j}}V(x_{j})\Big|\leq(C_{V}k)^{k}

for any sequence of multi-indices
\alpha_{j}
with

0\leq|\alpha_{j}|\leq 20d
.

Alternatively,
V
is also admissible if it is Gaussian
and stationary with a two-point correlation function

{\mathbf{E}\,}V(x)V(y)=R(x-y)
satisfying
\supp R\subset B_{2r}
and

|\partial^{\beta}R(x)|\leq C(1+|x|)^{-d-1-|\beta|},

for multi-indices of order
0\leq|\beta|\leq 20d
.

Aside from Gaussian potentials, examples of admissible potentials include
potentials constructed from a point process, such as

V(y)=\sum_{x\in X}V_{0}(y-x)

with a fixed smooth compactly supported potential

V_{0}
satisfying
\int V_{0}=0
, where
X
is (for example) a Poisson
process with unit intensity.

Another example of an admissible potential is the lattice construction

V(y)=\sum_{x\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}}c_{x}V_{0}(y-x-h),

where
V_{0}
is a smooth and compactly supported function and
c_{x}

are independent Bernoulli
\{\pm 1\}
coinflips, and
h\in[0,1]^{d}
is
a uniformly random shift (which makes
V
stationary).

Lemma B.3
.

If
V
is an admissible potential according
to Definition
B.2
, then

(B.7)

\begin{split}\Big|{\mathbf{E}\,}\prod_{j=1}^{k}\partial_{q}^{\alpha_{j}}{\widehat{V_{y_{j}}}}(q_{j})\Big|\leq r^{-dk}&r^{\sum_{j\in[k]}|\alpha_{j}|}\sum_{P\in{\mathcal{P}}([k])}\prod_{S\in P}(CC_{V}|S|)^{2|S|}r^{d}\exp\Big(-c_{k}\big|r\sum_{j\in S}q_{j}\big|^{0.99}\Big)\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\times\prod_{j\in[k]}(1+|q_{j}|)^{-20d}.\end{split}

for any sequence
y_{j}\in{\mathbf{R}}^{d}
.

Remark:

The estimate (
B.7
), along with stationarity, are the two
conditions we actually need for the proof. In particular, this implies
that independent sums of admissible potentials are also valid potentials for
the proof.

Proof.

We first expand the Fourier transform as follows:

\displaystyle{\mathbf{E}\,}\prod_{j=1}^{k}\partial^{\alpha_{j}}{\widehat{V_{y_{j}}}}(q_{j})

\displaystyle=\int{\mathbf{E}\,}\prod_{j\in[k]}V(x_{j})e^{-i\sum q_{j}\cdot x_{j}}\prod_{j=1}^{k}x_{j}^{\alpha_{j}}b_{r}(x_{j}-y_{j})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x_{1}\cdots\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x_{k}.

To split the expectation, let
P({\mathbf{x}})\in{\mathcal{P}}([k])
be the partition of
[k]

into connected components of the graph
G({\mathbf{x}})=\{(i,j)\mid|x_{i}-x_{j}|\leq 1\}
.
Then for any
P\geq P({\mathbf{x}})
,

\prod_{j=1}^{k}V(x_{j})=\prod_{S\in P}{\mathbf{E}\,}\prod_{j\in S}V(x_{j}).

We will use (
B.4
) and
Let
b\in C_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbf{R}}^{d})
be a smooth function
that is identical to
1
on the unit ball
B_{1}
, has support in
B_{2}
, and satisfies

|{\widehat{b}}(p)|\leq C\exp(-c|p|^{0.999}).

Then by (
B.4
), we have for any
\bm{x}\in({\mathbf{R}}^{d})^{k}

1=\sum_{F}\prod_{(i,j)\in F}b(x_{i}-x_{j})\prod_{(i,j)\not\in D(F)}(1-b(x_{i}-x_{j})).=\sum_{F}\phi_{F}(\bm{x}).

Note that, for
\bm{x}\in\supp\phi_{F}
,
P({\mathbf{x}})\leq P(F)
.
We use this decomposition in the integral,

\displaystyle{\mathbf{E}\,}\prod_{j=1}^{k}\partial^{\alpha_{j}}{\widehat{V_{y_{j}}}}(q_{j})

\displaystyle=\sum_{F}\int\phi_{F}({\mathbf{x}})\prod_{S\in P(F)}{\mathbf{E}\,}\prod_{j\in S}V(x_{j})e^{-i\sum q_{j}\cdot x_{j}}\prod_{j=1}^{k}x_{j}^{\alpha_{j}}b_{r}(x_{j}-y_{j})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\mathbf{x}}.

Next we use the identity (
B.5
) to write

\phi_{G}({\mathbf{x}})=\sum_{F^{\prime}\supset F}(-1)^{|F^{\prime}\setminus F|}\psi_{F^{\prime}}(\bm{x}),

where

\psi_{F}({\mathbf{x}})=\prod_{e\in F}b(x_{i}-x_{j})\prod_{e\in C(F)\setminus D(F)}(1-b(x_{i}-x_{j})).

Note that
\psi_{F}
can be expressed in the form

\psi_{F}({\mathbf{x}})=\prod_{S\in P(F)}(\prod_{e\in F|_{S}}b(x_{i}-x_{j})\prod_{e\in C(F|_{S})\setminus D(F)}(1-b(x_{i}-x_{j})))=:\prod_{S\in P(F)}\psi_{F,S}({\mathbf{x}}).

We have therefore arrived at the expression

\displaystyle{\mathbf{E}\,}\prod_{j=1}^{k}\partial^{\alpha_{j}}{\widehat{V_{y_{j}}}}(q_{j})

\displaystyle=\sum_{F\subset F^{\prime}}(-1)^{|F^{\prime}\setminus F|}\int\prod_{S\in P(F)}\psi_{F,S}({\mathbf{x}}){\mathbf{E}\,}\prod_{j\in S}V(x_{j})e^{-i\sum q_{j}\cdot x_{j}}\prod_{j=1}^{k}x_{j}^{\alpha_{j}}b_{r}(x_{j}-y_{j})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\mathbf{x}}.

We also need to introduce the following change of variables. Given the forest
F^{\prime}\geq F
,
which induces a partition

P^{\prime}=P(F^{\prime})\in{\mathcal{P}}([k])
, we define the variables
x_{S}=x_{\min S}
for each
S\in P^{\prime}
.
Also for each
j\in S
we set
z_{j}=x_{j}-x_{S}
. Let
S^{+}:=S\setminus\min\{S\}
, and define

{\mathbf{z}}_{S^{+}}=(z_{j})_{j\in S^{+}}
. By the stationarity of the potential
V
, we have

\prod_{S^{\prime}\subset S}{\mathbf{E}\,}\prod_{j\in S^{\prime}}V(x_{j})=\prod_{S^{\prime}\subset S}{\mathbf{E}\,}\prod_{j\in S^{\prime}}V(z_{j}),

where the product over
S^{\prime}
ranges over sets
S^{\prime}\in P(F)
.
Moreover, we have

\sum_{j\in S}x_{j}\cdot q_{j}=\big(\sum_{j\in S}q_{j}\big)\cdot x_{S}+\sum_{j\in S^{+}}q_{j}\cdot z_{j}.

Then applying the change of variables
(x_{j})_{j\in[k]}\mapsto((x_{S})_{S\in P(F^{\prime})},((z_{j})_{j\in S})_{S\in P(F^{\prime})})
,
we can write

\displaystyle{\mathbf{E}\,}\prod_{j=1}^{k}\partial^{\alpha_{j}}{\widehat{V_{y_{j}}}}(q_{j})

\displaystyle=\sum_{F\leq F^{\prime}}(-1)^{|F^{\prime}\setminus F|}\int\prod_{S\in P(F^{\prime})}\psi_{F^{\prime},S}({\mathbf{z}}_{S})e^{i\sum q_{j}\cdot z_{j}}\prod_{S\subset P(F)}{\mathbf{E}\,}\prod_{j\in S}V(z_{j})

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\prod_{S\in P^{\prime}(F)}\Big(\int e^{i(\sum_{j\in S}q_{j})\cdot x_{S}}\prod_{j\in S}(x_{S}+z_{j})^{\alpha_{j}}b_{r}(x_{S}+z_{j}-y_{j})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x_{S}\Big)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\mathbf{z}}_{P(F^{\prime})}.

The inner integral satisfies is the Fourier transform of the function

\prod_{j\in S}(x_{S}+z_{j})^{\alpha_{j}}b(x_{S}+x_{j}-y_{j}),

which is itself a product of
|S|
functions whose Fourier transform has decay
of the form
\exp(-c(r|p|)^{0.999})
. Thus

\big|\int e^{i(\sum_{j\in S}q_{j})\cdot x_{S}}\prod_{j\in S}(x_{S}+z_{j})^{\alpha_{j}}b_{r}(x_{S}+z_{j}-y_{j})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x_{S}\big|\leq\big(\prod_{j\in S}r^{\alpha_{j}}\big)r^{(1-|S|)d}\exp(-c(|S|^{-1}|\sum_{j\in S}q_{j}|)^{0.999}).

The factor
r^{(1-|S|)d}
comes from a factor of
r^{d}
which is the volume of the domain of integration
and a factor of
r^{-d}
from each function
b_{r}
(which are normalized so that
\int b_{r}=1
).
Taking a derivative of the integral with respect to the
z_{j}
variable at worst produces a factor of
\alpha_{j}

and also a factor of
r^{-1}
. Thus for
\beta_{j}\leq 20d
,

\big|\prod_{j\in S}\partial_{z_{j}}^{\beta_{j}}\int e^{i(\sum_{j\in S}q_{j})\cdot x_{S}}\prod_{j\in S}(x_{S}+z_{j})^{\alpha_{j}}b_{r}(x_{S}+z_{j}-y_{j})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x_{S}\big|\leq C\big(\prod_{j\in S}r^{\alpha_{j}}\big)r^{d}\exp(-c(|S|^{-1}|\sum_{j\in S}q_{j}|)^{0.999}).

To obtain the desired decay in the
q_{j}
variables we integrate by parts
20d
times in each
q_{j}

variable satisfying
|q_{j}|\geq 1
.

We therefore obtain a bound of the form

\displaystyle\big|

\displaystyle\int\prod_{S\in P(F^{\prime})}\psi_{F^{\prime},S}({\mathbf{z}}_{S})e^{i\sum q_{j}\cdot z_{j}}\prod_{S\subset P(F)}{\mathbf{E}\,}\prod_{j\in S}V(z_{j})

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\prod_{S\in P^{\prime}(F)}\Big(\int e^{i(\sum_{j\in S}q_{j})\cdot x_{S}}\prod_{j\in S}(x_{S}+z_{j})^{\alpha_{j}}b_{r}(x_{S}+z_{j}-y_{j})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x_{S}\Big)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\mathbf{z}}_{P(F^{\prime})}\big|

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad{\,\lesssim\,}r^{\sum|\alpha_{j}|}r^{-kd}\prod_{S\in P(F)}(C_{V}k)^{k}\prod_{j\in[k]}(1+|q_{j}|)^{-20d}\prod_{S\in P(F^{\prime})}r^{d}\exp(-c(|S|^{-1}|\sum_{j\in S}q_{j}|)^{0.999}).

We sum over all
F
and
F^{\prime}
with fixed partitions, observing that the number of forests having partition
P(F)=P

is given by
\prod_{S\in P}|S|^{|S|-2}
, by Cayley’s formula.
∎

Appendix C
Wavepackets and quantization

Throughout the paper we will use wavepackets to decompose functions, operators, and
quantum channels. Our starting point is the definition of a wavepacket. We will fix
a length scale
r={\varepsilon}^{-1}
and an envelope
\chi_{env}\in{\mathcal{S}}({\mathbf{R}}^{d})
which
is compactly supported in
B(0,1)
and satisfies the Fourier decay estimate

|{\widehat{\chi_{env}}}(p)|\leq C\exp(-c|p|^{0.9}).

We normalize
\chi_{env}
so that
\|\chi_{env}\|_{L^{2}}=1
.
Then given
(x_{0},p_{0})\in{{\mathbf{R}}^{2d}}
, we define the functions

\phi_{x_{0},p_{0}}(y)=r^{-d/2}e^{ip_{0}\cdot y}\chi_{env}(r^{-1}(y-x_{0})).

We will use greek letters like
\xi
and
\eta
to denote points in phase
space
{{\mathbf{R}}^{2d}}
and will write
\phi_{\xi}
for the wavepacket localized
to
\xi
. We also write
\mathinner{|{\xi}\rangle}
to denote the wavefunction
\phi_{\xi}
,
and use Dirac notation. This means that for example we may write

\mathinner{\langle{\xi|\psi}\rangle}
to mean
\langle\phi_{\xi},\psi\rangle
. For

p\in{\mathbf{R}}^{d}
we also use
\mathinner{|{p}\rangle}
and
\mathinner{\langle{p}|}
to denote the momentum
eigenfunctions
e^{ip\cdot x}
. Thus for example

\mathinner{\langle{p|\xi}\rangle}={\widehat{\phi_{\xi}}}(p)
.

For points
\xi=(\xi_{x},\xi_{p})
and

\eta=(\eta_{x},\eta_{p})
in phase space we use the metric

d_{r}(\xi,\eta):=r^{-1}|\xi_{x}-\eta_{x}|+r|\xi_{p}-\eta_{p}|.

This metric is the natural metric for comparing wavepackets at
scale
r
. The following bound holds for the overlap of wavepackets

(C.1)

|\mathinner{\langle{\xi|\eta}\rangle}|\leq\exp(-cd_{r}(\xi,\eta)^{0.9}).

To prove (
C.1
) observe that it suffices to consider

|\xi_{x}-\eta_{x}|\leq 2r
, as otherwise
\mathinner{\langle{\xi|\eta}\rangle}=0
. The
bound then follows from the Fourier decay estimate on
\chi_{env}
.

We will occasionally use the
one-parameter family of maps
U_{s}:{{\mathbf{R}}^{2d}}\to{{\mathbf{R}}^{2d}}

U_{s}(\xi)=(\xi_{x}+s\xi_{p},\xi_{p}).

In Dirac notation, the Fourier inversion formula is written

\Id=\int_{{\mathbf{R}}^{d}}\mathinner{|{p}\rangle}\mathinner{\langle{p}|}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}p.

An elementary calculation involving the Fourier inversion formula also
shows that we can decompose the identity operator into wavepackets as
follows

(C.2)

\Id=\int_{{{\mathbf{R}}^{2d}}}\mathinner{|{\xi}\rangle}\mathinner{\langle{\xi}|}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi.

This is equivalent to the identity

\langle f,g\rangle=\int_{{{\mathbf{R}}^{2d}}}\langle f,\phi_{\xi}\rangle\langle\phi_{\xi},g\rangle\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi.

As a consequence of (
C.2
) we have the following
expression for the
L^{2}
norm of a function
\psi\in L^{2}({\mathbf{R}}^{d})

(C.3)

\|\psi\|_{L^{2}}^{2}=\mathinner{\langle{\psi|\psi}\rangle}=\int\mathinner{\langle{\psi|\xi}\rangle}\mathinner{\langle{\xi|\psi}\rangle}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi=\int|\langle{\psi|\xi}\rangle|^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi.

The identity (
C.2
) can be used to decompose arbitrary
bounded operators
A\in{\mathcal{B}}(L^{2}({\mathbf{R}}^{d}))
.

(C.4)

\begin{split}A=\Id A\Id&=\int\mathinner{|{\xi}\rangle}\mathinner{\langle{\xi|A|\eta}\rangle}\mathinner{\langle{\eta}|}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\eta\\
&=\int\mathinner{|{\xi}\rangle}\mathinner{\langle{\eta}|}\mathinner{\langle{\xi|A|\eta}\rangle}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\eta.\end{split}

In the last line we simply rearranged
\mathinner{\langle{\xi|A|\eta}\rangle}
, which
is the scalar
\mathinner{\langle{\phi_{\xi},A\phi_{\eta}}\rangle}
and therefore commutes
with multiplication by
\mathinner{\langle{\eta}|}
.

In this paper we will be working with operators more so than
wavefunctions. We will primarily bound operators using the operator
norm, and we use the following version of the Schur test to estimate
the operator norm.

Lemma C.1

(Schur test in the wavepacket frame)
.

Let
A\in{\mathcal{B}}(L^{2}({\mathbf{R}}^{d}))
be an operator of the form

A=\int\mathinner{|{\xi}\rangle}\mathinner{\langle{\eta}|}a(\xi,\eta)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\eta.

Then

\|A\|_{op}^{2}\leq C\big(\sup_{\xi}\int|a(\xi,\eta)|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\eta\big)\big(\sup_{\eta}\int|a(\xi,\eta)|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi\big)

Proof.

Let
\psi\in L^{2}({\mathbf{R}}^{d})
. The first step is to expand,
using (
C.3
),

\displaystyle\|A\psi\|_{L^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\int|\langle{\xi|A\psi}\rangle|^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi

\displaystyle=\int\big|\int\langle{\xi|\xi^{\prime}}\rangle\mathinner{\langle{\eta|\psi}\rangle}a(\xi^{\prime},\eta)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi^{\prime}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\eta\big|^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi.

For simplicity now define

b(\xi,\eta)=\int\mathinner{\langle{\xi|\xi^{\prime}}\rangle}a(\xi^{\prime},\eta)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi^{\prime}
. Then,
using the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality we write

\displaystyle\|A\psi\|_{L^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\int\big(\int|\langle{\eta|\psi}\rangle|^{2}|b(\xi,\eta)|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\eta\big)\big(\int|b(\xi,\eta)|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\eta\big)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{\xi}\int|b(\xi,\eta)|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\eta\int\int|\langle{\eta|\psi}\rangle|^{2}|b(\xi,\eta)|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\eta

\displaystyle\leq(\sup_{\xi}\int|b(\xi,\eta)|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\eta)(\sup_{\eta}\int|b(\xi,\eta)|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)\|\psi\|_{L^{2}}^{2}.

It remains to show that

\int|b(\xi,\eta)|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\eta\leq C\sup_{\xi}\int|a(\xi,\eta)|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\eta.

and

\int|b(\xi,\eta)|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi\leq C\sup_{\eta}\int|a(\xi,\eta)|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi.

The first follows from

\displaystyle\int|b(\xi,\eta)|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\eta

\displaystyle=\int|\langle{\xi|\xi^{\prime}}\rangle||a(\xi^{\prime},\eta)|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi^{\prime}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\eta

\displaystyle\leq\int|\langle{\xi|\xi^{\prime}}\rangle|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi^{\prime}\sup_{\xi}^{\prime}\int|a(\xi^{\prime},\eta)|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\eta,

and the second from an analogous calculation.
∎

The Weyl quantization map
\Op^{w}
takes as input a classical
observable
a\in C^{\infty}({{\mathbf{R}}^{2d}})
and produces an operator

\Op^{w}(a)
defined by

\Op^{w}(a)f(x):=\int e^{i(x-y)\cdot p}a(\frac{x+y}{2},p)f(y)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}p.

We define rescaled
C^{k}
norms for phase space as follows

\|a\|_{C_{r}^{k}({{\mathbf{R}}^{2d}})}:=\sum_{|\alpha|\leq k}\sup_{\xi\in{{\mathbf{R}}^{2d}}}|(r\partial_{x})^{\alpha_{x}}(r^{-1}\partial_{p})^{\alpha_{p}}a(\xi)|.

Using this norm we derive the following bounds for the Weyl
quantization map.

Lemma C.2
.

If
a\in C^{k}({{\mathbf{R}}^{2d}})
then

(C.5)

|\langle{\xi|\Op^{w}(a)|\eta}\rangle|\leq C(1+d_{r}(\xi,\eta))^{-k}\|a\|_{C^{k+d+1}_{r}}.

Proof.

First we write out explicitly

\displaystyle\mathinner{\langle{\xi|\Op^{w}(a)|\eta}\rangle}=r^{-d}\int e^{i[x\cdot(p-\xi_{p})+y\cdot(\eta_{p}-p)]}a(\frac{x+y}{2},p)\chi_{env}(r^{-1}(x-\xi_{x}))\chi_{env}(r^{-1}(y-\eta_{x}))\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}p\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x.

There are several cases to consider. The first case is that

d_{r}(\xi,\eta)\leq K
. In this case we simply aim to show that
the integral is bounded by a constant. To do this we will split up
the integral over the
p
variable as follows.
Let
\rho\in C_{c}^{\infty}
be a smooth cutoff function that
is supported in
B(0,2r^{-1})
and is identically
1
on the ball

B(0,r^{-1})
. We decompose the integration over
p
using
the identity

1=(1-\rho(p-\xi_{p}))+\rho(p-\xi_{p})(1-\rho(p-\eta_{p}))+\rho(p-\xi_{p})\rho(p-\eta_{p}).

In the second and third terms, we can use localization to a ball
of radius
r^{-1}
in
p
and simply apply the triangle inequality
to bound the integral by a constant
C
.
For the first term we integrate by parts
d+1
times in the variable
x

to obtain integrable decay in the
p
variable outside the ball

B(\xi_{p},r^{-1})
.

The second case is that
d_{r}(\xi,\eta)\geq K
and

r^{-1}|\xi_{x}-\eta_{x}|\geq\frac{1}{2}d_{r}(\xi,\eta)
.
In this case we integrate by parts
k
times in the
p
variable to obtain
decay in
|x-y|
, using
|x-y|\geq\frac{1}{4}|\xi_{x}-\eta_{x}|

on the support of the envelopes. An additional integration by parts of
order
d+1
is required in the
x
variable to obtain the required
localization in the
p
variable as described above.

The final case is that
d_{r}(\xi,\eta)\geq K
and

r|\xi_{p}-\eta_{p}|\geq\frac{1}{2}d_{r}(\xi,\eta)
. In this case
we perform the change of
variables
(x,y)\mapsto(\overline{x},\Delta x):=(\frac{x+y}{2},x-y)
and integrate by parts in the
\overline{x}

variable
k
times. A final integration by parts
d+1
times
in the
\Delta x
variable is required to obtain integrability in the

p
variable.
∎

We note that combining Lemma
C.2
and
Lemma
C.1
provides an alternative proof of the
Calderón-Vaillancourt theorem on the
L^{2}
boundedness
of pseudodifferential operators.

We conclude this section by comparing the Weyl quantization to the wavepacket
quantization
\Op(a)
which we define as

\Op(a):=\int\mathinner{|{\xi}\rangle}\mathinner{\langle{\xi}|}a(\xi)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi.

The key relationship between these quantizations is that the Wigner
function of the wavpeacket envelope,
W_{env}(x,p):={\mathcal{W}_{\chi_{env}}}(x,p)
,
satisfies

\Op^{w}(W_{env})=\mathinner{|{(0,0)}\rangle}\mathinner{\langle{(0,0)}|}.

Then applying translation and modulation symmetries of the Wigner function
and of the wavepacket frame, this implies

\Op^{w}(W_{env}(\cdot-x_{0},\cdot-p_{0}))=\mathinner{|{(x_{0},p_{0})}\rangle}\mathinner{\langle{(x_{0},p_{0})}|}.

Therefore

\Op(a)=\Op^{w}(a\ast W_{env}),

so

\|\Op(a)-\Op^{w}(a)\|_{op}\leq\|a-a\ast W_{env}\|_{C^{2d+1}_{r}}.

Then using the fundamental theorem of calculus on each partial derivative we
obtain the following bound for
\delta<1
:

\|a-a(\cdot-x_{0},\cdot-p_{0})\|_{C^{2d+1}_{r}}\leq(\delta(r^{-1}|x_{0}|+r|p_{0}|)+\delta^{2d+1})\|a\|_{C^{2d+1}_{r,\delta^{-1}}}.

Then, since
W_{env}(x,p)
is localized to the region

|x|{\,\lesssim\,}r
and
|p|{\,\lesssim\,}r^{-1}
, we conclude

\|a-a\ast W_{env}\|_{C^{2d+1}_{r}}\leq C\delta\|a\|_{C^{2d+1}_{r,\delta^{-1}}}.

Therefore we conclude

(C.6)

\|\Op(a)-\Op^{w}(a)\|_{op}\leq C\delta\|a\|_{C^{2d+1}_{r,\delta^{-1}}}.

Appendix D
Elementary estimates for the linear Boltzmann equation

In this section we prove some simple estimates for solutions to the linear
Boltzmann equation

(D.1)

\partial_{t}f+p\cdot\nabla_{x}f=Lf,

where
L
is the scattering operator

Lf(x,p)={\varepsilon}^{2}\int\delta(|p|^{2}-|q|^{2})G(p-q)[f(x,q)-f(x,p)]\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}q.

We are interested in rescaled
C^{k}
estimates for the solution. Recall
the
C^{k}_{r,L}
norm is defined by

\|f\|_{C^{k}_{r,L}}:=\sum_{|\alpha_{x}|+|\alpha_{p}|\leq k}\sup_{x,p}|(rL\partial_{x})^{\alpha_{x}}(r^{-1}L\partial_{p})^{\alpha_{p}}f(x,p)|.

This norm measures the smoothness of the function
f
to scale
Lr
in space and
Lr^{-1}
in momentum.

To solve (
D.1
) we use a series expansion

(D.2)

f_{t}=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}g_{t,j}

where

g_{t,0}(x,p)=f_{0}(x-tp,p)

and

(D.3)

g_{t,j+1}(x,p)=\int_{0}^{t}(Lg_{t-s,j})(x-sp,p)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s.

The partial sums

f_{t,N}:=\sum_{j=0}^{N}g_{t,j}

solve

\partial_{t}f_{t,N}+p\cdot\nabla_{x}f_{t,N}=Lf_{t,N-1}.

Our task is to show that the terms
g_{t,j}
are summable in
C^{k}_{r,L}
for sufficiently small
t
.
We will prove the following result.

Lemma D.1
.

For every
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
, and dimension
d\geq 2
, there exists a constant
C=C(k,d)
such that the following holds:
If
\supp f\subset\{(x,p)|\mid|p|\geq\theta\}

and
T\leq C^{-1}\delta^{-1}\min\{r^{-2},{\varepsilon}^{2}\theta^{k-1}\}
then the
series (
D.2
) converges and the terms
g_{j,t}
satisfy

\|g_{j,t}\|_{C^{k}_{r,\delta L}}\leq(C{\varepsilon}^{2}t\theta^{1-k})^{j}\|f\|_{C^{k}_{r,L}}.

In particular, it follows that, for
t\leq T
,

\|f_{t}\|_{C^{k}_{r,\delta L}}\leq C\|f\|_{C^{k}_{r,L}}.

Moreover

\|f_{t}-f_{t,1}\|_{C^{k}_{r,L}}\leq C({\varepsilon}^{2}t\delta\theta^{1-k})^{2}\|f\|_{C^{k}_{r,L}}.

This reduces to demonstrating bounds for the scattering operator
L
in the
C^{k}_{r,L}
norm.
To do this we will treat the radial and tangential derivatives separately, which is why we require an
assumption that
f
is supported away from
|p|=0
.

D.1.
The
C^{k}
norm in polar coordinates

Given a vector field
V\in C^{\infty}({\mathbf{R}}^{d})
we define the derivative operator

D_{V}f=V(p)\cdot\nabla_{p}f.

We say that a vector field
V
is tangential if
p\cdot V(p)=0
and we write
R(p)=p/|p|
for the radial
unit vector field.

Lemma D.2
.

Let
f\in C^{\infty}({\mathbf{R}}^{d}\setminus B_{\theta})
. Then for any multi-index
|\alpha|=k
,

|\partial^{\alpha}f(x,p)|\leq C_{k,d}\sup_{W_{1},\cdots,W_{k}}|D_{W_{1}}D_{W_{2}}\cdots D_{W_{k}}f(x,p)|+\sum_{j<k}\theta^{j-k}\|f\|_{C^{j}}.

where each vector field
W_{j}
is either tangential or is the radial vector field
R
,
and the tangential vector fields
W_{j}
are smooth on the unit sphere and are
0
-homogeneous.

Proof.

The proof is by induction, with the case
k=1
following from the fact that for any
p
one can construct an
orthonormal basis from the radial vector
p/|p|
and a collection of
d-1
tangential unit vectors, and the bound

\|v\|\leq C_{d}\max_{1\leq j\leq d}|v\cdot e_{j}|

which holds for any orthonormal basis
e_{j}
.

To induct we observe that

D_{W_{1}}D_{W_{2}}\cdots D_{W_{k}}f=\sum_{j_{1},\cdots,j_{k}=1}^{d}\prod_{i=1}^{k}W_{i,j_{i}}(\partial_{j_{1}}\partial_{j_{2}}\cdots\partial_{j_{k}}f)+\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}E_{j}[W_{1},\cdots,W_{k}]f(p),

where the remainder term
E_{j}[W_{1},\cdots,W_{k}]f
involves a sum of lower order terms in which
f
is differentiated

j
times and
k-j
derivatives of the vector fields
W
appear. On the set
{\mathbf{R}}^{d}\setminus B_{\theta}
,
the vector fields satisfy

|\partial^{\alpha}W|\leq C\theta^{-|\alpha|}

by
0
-homogeneity. In particular we conclude that

\big|\sum_{j_{1},\cdots,j_{k}=1}^{d}\prod_{i=1}^{k}W_{i,j_{i}}(\partial_{j_{1}}\partial_{j_{2}}\cdots\partial_{j_{k}}f)\big|\leq|D_{W_{1}}D_{W_{2}}\cdots D_{W_{k}}f|+C_{k}\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}\theta^{j-k}\|f\|_{C^{j}}.

The result follows from summing over all choices of tangential and radial derivatives (fixing a basis of tangential
derivatives).
∎

To control higher order derivatives of the form
D_{W_{1}}\cdots D_{W_{k}}
applied to the scattering operator
L

we need to first work out some exact computations. Define the generalized scattering operator with kernel
H

to be

L_{H}f(p)=\int\delta(|p|^{2}-|q|^{2})H(p,q)[f(q)-f(p)]\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}q.

For convenience, we split this into an “incoming” part
L_{H}^{+}
and an “outgoing” part

L_{H}^{-}
, with

L_{H}^{+}(p)=\int\delta(|p|^{2}-|q|^{2})H(p,q)f(q)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}q

and

L_{H}^{-}(p)=-\int\delta(|p|^{2}-|q|^{2})H(p,q)f(p)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}q.

Lemma D.3
.

If
H
is a
C^{\infty}
scattering kernel and
V
is a tangential vector field, we have the identities

(D.4)

\displaystyle D_{R}L_{H}f

\displaystyle=L_{H}D_{R}f+L_{H_{R}}f

(D.5)

\displaystyle D_{V}L_{H}f

\displaystyle=L_{H}^{-}D_{V}f+L_{D_{V}[p]H}f

where

H_{R}(p,q)=(\frac{p}{|p|}\cdot\nabla_{p}+\frac{q}{|q|}\cdot\nabla_{q}+d)H(p,q).

and

D_{V}[p]H(p,q)=p\cdot\nabla_{p}H(p,q).

Proof.

For the radial derivative, we start with the observation

p\cdot\nabla_{p}\delta(|p|^{2}-|q|^{2})=-q\cdot\nabla_{q}\delta(|p|^{2}-|q|^{2}).

On integrating by parts we have

\displaystyle D_{R}Lf

\displaystyle=D_{R}[p](\int\delta(|p|^{2}-|q|^{2})H(p-q)[f(q)-f(p)]\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}q)

\displaystyle=\int\delta(|p|^{2}-|q|^{2})(D_{R}[p]H)(p-q)[f(q)-f(p)]\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}q

\displaystyle\qquad+\int\frac{1}{|p|}\delta(|p|^{2}-|q|^{2})\nabla_{q}\cdot(q(H(p-q)[f(q)-f(p)]))\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}q

\displaystyle\qquad-\int\delta(|p|^{2}-|q|^{2})H(p-q)D_{R}f(p)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}q

\displaystyle=L_{D_{R}[p]H}f+L_{H}^{-}(D_{R}f)+\int\frac{1}{|p|}\delta(|p|^{2}-|q|^{2})\nabla_{q}\cdot(q(H(p-q)[f(q)-f(p)]))\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}q.

The calculation then follows from applying the product rule.

For the tangential derivative, we use the fact that

V(p)\cdot\nabla_{p}\delta(|p|^{2}-|q|^{2})=0

for tangential vector fields
V
. Then (
D.5
) follows
from a direct calculation.
∎

We are now ready to prove our main bound.

Lemma D.4
.

Let
H
be a
C^{\infty}
scattering kernel satisfying

(D.6)

|\partial^{\alpha}H(p,q)|\leq C_{\alpha}A\theta^{-|\alpha|},

and suppose that
f
is a function supported in
\{|p|\geq\theta\}
. Then for
k\geq 1
,

\|L_{H}f\|_{C^{k}}\leq AC_{k}\theta^{1-m}\|f\|_{C^{k}}.

Proof.

The proof is by induction on
k
. When
k=1
this follows from Lemma
D.2
and
Lemma
D.3
.
For larger
k
it suffices to show that

\|D_{W_{1}}\cdots D_{W_{k}}L_{H}f\|_{C^{k}}\leq\theta^{1-k}\|f\|_{C^{k}}.

for any mixture of tangential and radial vector fields
W_{1},\cdots,W_{k}
.
To induct we use Lemma
D.3
.
If
W_{k}
is radial then

D_{W_{1}}\cdot D_{W_{k-1}}D_{W_{k}}L_{H}f=D_{W_{1}}\cdot D_{W_{k-1}}L_{H}(D_{W_{k}}f)+D_{W_{1}}\cdot D_{W_{k-1}}L_{H_{R}}f

whereas if
W_{k}
is tangential

D_{W_{1}}\cdot D_{W_{k-1}}D_{W_{k}}L_{H}f=D_{W_{1}}\cdot D_{W_{k-1}}L^{-}_{H}(D_{W_{k}}f)+D_{W_{1}}\cdot D_{W_{k-1}}L_{D_{W_{k}}[p]H}f.

Both terms are bounded using the inductive hypothesis, observing
that if
H
satisfies (
D.6
) for some
A
then

D_{W_{k}}H
and
H_{R}
satisfy (
D.6
) with constant
A\theta^{-1}
.
∎

D.2.
Convergence of the series

Before we address the convergence of the series (
D.2
) we quickly observe that the free
transport operator

T_{s}f(x,p):=f(x-sp,p)

satisfies the bound

\|T_{s}f\|_{C^{k}_{r,L}}\leq(1+Csr^{-2})\|f\|_{C^{k}_{r,L}}.

Using
T_{s}
we can rewrite the iteration (
D.3
) defining
g_{j}
as follows:

g_{j+1,t}=\int_{0}^{t}T_{s}Lg_{j,t-s}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s.

Then applying the triangle inequality and Lemma
D.4
we have

\|g_{j+1,t}\|_{C^{k}_{r}}\leq Ct{\varepsilon}^{2}(1+Ctr^{-2})\theta^{1-k}\sup_{0\leq s\leq t}\|g_{j,t}\|_{C^{k}_{r}}.

This concludes the proof of Lemma
D.1
.
